“Software is over”: Bold AI developer takes aim at Adobe with open source clones
2026-10-07

“Software is over”: Bold AI developer takes aim at Adobe with open source clones

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
For a while, those hoping to avoid the difficulty and expense of dealing with Adobe’s Creative Suite have been able to use several free and/or open source alternatives that replicate some or all of those capabilities. Now, one developer is using AI-powered reverse engineering to mimic the look, feel, and functionality of Adobe’s well-known software with what he hopes will soon be perfect fidelity. Artcraft got its start last year as a “controllable AI for artists” that allows for precise post-generation editing of AI-crafted images and videos. Over the weekend, though, the brand debuted its pivot to a suite of seven open source apps recreating the user interface and tools found in Adobe’s Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere, Lightroom, After Effects, InDesign, and Acrobat Pro. In announcing those new apps on Reddit earlier this week, software developer Brandon Thomas said he used Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5.5 to create “clean-room replacements” for Adobe’s closed source software in Rust (with WebAssembly versions available for use in a browser). And while commenters on Hacker News and elsewhere have taken pains to point out the new software’s many current shortcomings, Thomas makes the grandiose promise that the current “super early alpha” state will soon lead to something just as functional as anything Adobe has ever put out. “We’re going to reach 100% [feature] parity within a month,” Thomas stated on Reddit of his plans to quickly squash bugs and add features with the help of community reports. Days later, on Hacker News, he amended that position slightly to say that “99% parity will take a while, but I’m sure it’ll be measured in months and not years.” Thomas, who says he has about a decade of experience working on large-scale Rust projects, wrote on Hacker News that he was inspired to create his own open source Adobe software alternatives after being “bit by the dark pattern ‘cancellation fee’ one too many times.” Development of the totally free options offered here under MIT/Apache licenses will be funded, Thomas wrote, by selling token-based access to the pre-existing Artcraft visual AI model and IDE, which is offered as an imagery-generation option inside the apps alongside third-party models. This revenue stream will help ensure these apps aren’t “going to be some orphaned small-team project,” Thomas wrote. “We have a team building popular software for filmmakers, so supporting this is fully funded.” Thomas is the latest in a long line of coders using AI tools to speed up the long-standing but labor-intensive practice of reverse engineering (i.e., replicating the functionality of a piece of software without directly copying the code itself). While that practice has generally been found legally acceptable, the ArtCraft apps could still face legal trouble if their “trade dress” too closely mimics Adobe’s familiar appearance and interface. After coding exclusively with AI tools since February, Thomas said he foresees a near future when everyday developers can re-create open source versions of popular paid software to “take back the internet and own tech forever.” “Now you can just create 1:1 functional equivalents, clean room, that are highly performant and cross-platform. Nothing is stopping you,” Thomas wrote. “We’ll replace Google Search, we’ll build our own smartphones, we’ll build better infra. We’ll remove all the ads and de-enshittify the entire tech world.” “Software is over,” he gloated in an earlier comment, claiming to have “one-shotted Photoshop. The whole thing.”
Ars Technica - All content
PA measles outbreak tops 1,000 cases, largest since disease was eliminated
2026-10-07

PA measles outbreak tops 1,000 cases, largest since disease was eliminated

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Pennsylvania’s explosive measles outbreak is now the largest the US has seen since the highly infectious virus was declared eliminated in 2000. With well over 1,000 cases, it’s the largest on record since 1991. Pennsylvania’s health officials reported 1,078 measles cases in all of 2026 as of this afternoon. That includes 12 cases in January and March, with the remaining 1,066 cases since April, when the current outbreak began. The tally includes 145 cases recorded in the last seven days and 74 identified since the previous update on Monday. Health officials noted that 40 of the 74 cases added today were from more than two weeks ago and are just now being recorded. Among the cases, there have been 207 hospitalizations and five deaths—a newborn, a 6-week-old, an 18-year-old, a 20-year-old, and a 40-year-old. All of the deaths occurred during the current outbreak in people who were unvaccinated; the two infants were too young to have been vaccinated. With slipping vaccination rates nationwide, measles outbreaks and cases have come roaring back in the past few years. A large outbreak in Texas that spanned January­–August 2025 ended with 762 cases, while an outbreak in South Carolina ended in April with 997 cases, the previous record holder. Pennsylvania exceeds that handily with its 1,066 outbreak-linked cases as of today. With 34 new infections reported since Monday, the outbreak does not appear to be close to ending. Measles was declared eliminated in the US in 2000 after decades-long vaccination efforts. Elimination is earned and maintained with vaccination rates that prevent continuous transmission of the virus during a 12-month period. In November, experts at the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO)—which acts as a regional office for the World Health Organization—will review data on whether the US has lost its measles elimination status. Experts widely expect the US has lost the status. Canada lost its elimination status in 2025. The last measles outbreak that was larger than the current one in Pennsylvania was an outbreak in New York, which spanned 1990–1991 and infected more than 4,500 in all. Earlier this week, New York Governor Kathy Hochul declared a State Disaster Emergency over rising measles cases there. There have been at least 108 cases in the state. The emergency declaration expands the pool of healthcare workers who can provide measles vaccinations, newly including paramedics, advanced EMS providers, pharmacists, and midwives. It also allows nurses, in addition to physicians, to order measles tests. “No one should get seriously ill or die from a vaccine-preventable disease,” Hochul said in a statement. “That is why I have taken steps to increase the number of health professionals who can vaccinate and test.” The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is reporting 3,887 measles cases nationwide for the year. That is the highest tally in the US since 1991, when there were 9,643 cases. Experts are becoming yet more anxious as measles outbreaks stretch into the fall; in the pre-vaccine era, measles was typically a virus of the late winter and early spring. Experts are concerned that spread could accelerate further in favorable conditions.

Financial insiders are buying their own stock at the slowest pace in nearly 23 years—a ‘negative data point’ just as bank earnings kick off
2026-10-08

Financial insiders are buying their own stock at the slowest pace in nearly 23 years—a ‘negative data point’ just as bank earnings kick off

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
As Wall Street’s biggest banks prepare for Q3 earnings starting next week, in one sense things have never looked better. Trading desks have been setting records. Blockbuster public offerings like SpaceX in June have also given bankers plenty reason to celebrate. But are the people who know the business best starting to have doubts about how long the run can last? A new report from VerityData showed that the number of financial executives buying shares of their own companies fell to a nearly 23-year low during the July-to-September quarter. According to Ben Silverman, head of research at VerityData, there were just 298 “unique financial-sector buyers,” or employees who made at least one purchase of their company’s stock, in Q3. That was the lowest in the financial research firm’s records, which go all the way back to 2004, and also just under the prior record low of 302 buyers in the third quarter of 2024. The number of sellers was somewhat higher, but not by an unusual amount. The report factors in insider trading activity across more than 3,000 financial services companies. That ranges from big banks to asset managers to insurers. This mirrored the pattern across the broader stock market. Insider buying fell 18% to 1,290 buyers, down from 1,580 in the second quarter. But why does this matter? At a glance, securities filings show that executives at JPMorgan routinely unload shares worth upwards of $800,000 in a quarter. A little over a month ago, a Goldman insider also sold more than $600,000 worth of company stock. Sometimes investors watch insider trading for clues about how those closest to a company view its business. Buying shares can mean management is more confident in a company’s future. Selling, however, can be harder to interpret. Instead, executives may sell shares to cover taxes, diversify their holdings or through prearranged trading plans. Jesse Fried, a Harvard Law School professor, believes fewer financial executives buying their own shares can indicate “a bearish signal for that sector.” That’s because “inside purchases by executives tend to predict future market-beating returns for their firms,” Fried told Fortune in an emailed statement. Meanwhile, VerityData’s Silverman says the lack of buying suggests insiders are “skittish” about valuations. “When equity prices get to a point that insiders feel that there’s a price dislocation between where the market’s perceiving a valuation, and where they believe it to be, they’ll hold off on buying,” Silverman added. “Like other investors, they’re not trying to chase stocks and they’re not trying to buy something they think is perhaps above fair value.” Not everyone agrees that investors should even factor in insider activity for the sector. “I’m very skeptical that this means anything,” Nejat Seyhun, a finance professor at the University of Michigan Ross School of Business, told Fortune. “When you look at how predictive insider trading is in the financial sector, it isn’t predictive at all.” Although insider trading generally can have a correlation with future returns in the market broadly, Seyhun says banks are among the weakest areas for that kind of prediction. That’s because bank stocks are particularly difficult to forecast, given that their performance depends heavily on macro factors rather than information that management might have about the company. He pointed to changes in monetary policy, interest rates and inflation levels. “Those are the kinds of things that affect the banking sector the most, and that’s not the kind of information an executive can have,” Seyhun added. “So, as a result, it’s very difficult for them to predict future stock returns in their own companies.” It’s a lot for investors to digest going into earnings. JPMorgan and Goldman Sachs will post third quarter results on Oct. 13. Morgan Stanley will follow suit on Oct. 14. Though profits and revenue growth have generally been strong this year, the IPO market has stalled, with Oura and Bamboo Insurance delaying their offerings last month. The Fed raised interest rates around the same time, which can make corporate dealmaking more difficult. Not to mention companies are taking out massive loans for their buzzy AI plans. That borrowing spree, in turn, could present a risk to large Wall Street firms if clients are unable to pay them back. Bank stocks have been underperforming the market recently, too. As of Wednesday, the KBW bank index is down nearly 11% over the past month, bucking the S&P 500’s 1% advance. The financial index is still up 3% so far in 2026. “That’s something to have in your cons column as people, or as investors, are preparing to digest those earnings and understand them,” he added. “And if they’re looking to position ahead of it, then it’s certainly a negative data point.” Morgan Chittum is a reporter at Fortune covering finance. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. ‘Lipstick on a pig’: Trump’s red-dyed diesel plan is more midterms appeal than gas price solution, experts say—and even truckers are calling his bluff
2026-10-08

‘Lipstick on a pig’: Trump’s red-dyed diesel plan is more midterms appeal than gas price solution, experts say—and even truckers are calling his bluff

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Diesel fuel prices are hovering at record highs above $6 per gallon amid the ongoing Iran war, but both energy analysts and trucking industry groups say the Trump administration’s new strategy is less a viable solution—and more of an act of political posturing. President Donald Trump signed an executive order this week deferring taxes on the use of red-dyed diesel, a type of fuel typically only used by farmers and truckers. Because this type of fuel is exempt from highway fuel taxes, it is illegal to use on public roads.  The red dye also allows a road inspector to detect whether a trucker is evading taxes by using the fuel on highways. The tax deferral, which would theoretically increase the supply of fuel truckers had access to by temporarily relieving the tax burden of 24.4 cents-per-gallon, is in place through the end of the year. Rather than rejoice at the prospect of more fuel with fewer strings attached, experts and stakeholders don’t see many advantages to the change. Instead, they contend, it’s a way for the Trump administration to try to save face as approval ratings continue to sink. A Reuters/Ipsos poll published this week revealed a 32% approval rating for the president, with Americans citing cost of living as their top concern. Trump has floated a suspension of the federal gas tax ahead of the midterm elections, though the move would require Congressional approval, and it has drawn widespread criticism over concerns of it creating an additional debt burden, as the tax revenue would have to come from elsewhere in the federal budget. Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, sees the red-dyed diesel tax deferral as a way for the White House to bypass Congress while appearing to the public as if it is addressing concerns over rocketing fuel costs. “This dyed diesel waiver—it doesn’t add supply globally. It doesn’t improve the reasons that have led to high prices,” De Haan told Fortune. “It would be more akin to lipstick on a pig.” White House Spokesperson Taylor Rogers said in a statement to Fortune the executive action “will quickly cut diesel costs and put money directly back into the pockets of American truckers,” and save them more than $100 per fuel refill. Chief among the concerns from industry stakeholders is that a tax deferral is not the same as a tax break, likely leaving truckers or drivers to still have to pay for the use of red-dyed diesel down the line. “We do not expect most reputable diesel retailers and fuel marketers to do this,” the Society of Independent Gasoline Marketers of America and the National Association of Truck Stop Owners said in a joint statement to their members. “First, the tax is still owed, so there’s limited upside.” David Russell, global head of market strategy at TradeStation Group, explained that deferrals are leaving truckers on edge because of the possibility they will still have to pay the taxes, just down the line. Trump has asked the Treasury to look for ways to eliminate the deferral, but the agency has not provided any guidance. “You have an unusual situation where they’re basically saying, ‘We’re going to try to not enforce a tax for a period of time, and we’re going to hope that Congress later blesses that action,’” Russell told Fortune. “And if not, then we might be on the hook, or we might create a situation where gas stations need to pay that tax back to the government later.’ So it creates a lot of uncertainty.” Even without the potential tax concerns, red-dyed diesel makes up only a fraction of the total fuel—about 30%—used by commercial vehicles, De Haan argued. Because red-dyed diesel is typically only used for specific contexts like farming, and isn’t widely available at many truck stops. The White House said more than 4,000 retailers in the U.S. distribute dyed diesel. “It’s rarely a truck stop fuel,” he said. “Even if you wanted to find this, it may be kind of like a diamond in the rough to find.” Analysts agreed that solving the U.S.’s fuel shortage would require larger geopolitical stabilization, such as the ending of the wars in Iran and Ukraine. Russell said that if the worst of the conflict in the Middle East were over, bottlenecks could ease—and combined with the few disruptions to the U.S.’s domestic oil production as a result of a mellow hurricane season so far, that could mean diesel prices could actually ease on their own. “The real solution to this situation is ultimately the supply and demand in the market,” Russell said. “It would not be because of this measure with red diesel. It would be a result of the improving situation and normal seasonal dynamics.” De Haan, however, fears a potential future spike in gas prices if Ukraine repeats its attack on Russian oil refineries—which is what Trump has attributed rising gas prices to—and suggested the best bet to increase global oil supply is for the president to keep his word to end the war in Ukraine. Otherwise, the red-dyed diesel tax deferral would have limited impact because actual fuel supply would not increase. “Aside from solving the underlying geopolitical issues, I don’t know that there’s a great, quick win here,” De Haan said. “The president is scrambling ahead of the midterms to do something, but I just don’t know that this is really a needle mover in my mind.” Sasha Rogelberg is a reporter and former editorial fellow on the news desk at Fortune, covering retail and the intersection of business and popular culture. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. ‘Where you come from shouldn’t dictate where you finish’: How Standard Chartered is taking on the banking industry’s class problem
2026-10-08

‘Where you come from shouldn’t dictate where you finish’: How Standard Chartered is taking on the banking industry’s class problem

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Darren Ellis and his older brother were the first in their family to go to university. It didn’t take him long to notice that many of his classmates came from very different backgrounds. “If it wasn’t for the fact I played sport at a high level, I’m not sure I would have had the confidence to interact with the people on my course,” says Ellis, now Standard Chartered’s chief financial officer for Europe, the Americas, Africa, and the Middle East. “Other people won’t have that.” His mum worked in a school kitchen, his dad in a factory. He understood the value of money from an early age. “If I broke something, it did not get replaced,” he says. It left him with a firm belief in effort. “Hard work is the bedrock of everything that we do. People who work hard should be given the opportunity,” he adds. Standard Chartered ranks No. 84 on the Fortune 100 Best Companies to Work For–Europe 2026 list. Since taking over as U.K. chair of its socioeconomic mobility working group in January 2026, Ellis has encouraged the bank to treat class background with the same rigor it applies to gender or ethnicity. “Where you come from shouldn’t dictate where you finish,” he says. In the U.K. financial services sector, employees from lower-income backgrounds take 16% longer than peers from higher socioeconomic backgrounds to reach senior roles, according to 2025 research by the Bridge Group and Progress Together, and only 26% of senior leaders come from lower socioeconomic backgrounds. This is despite the City of London’s socioeconomic diversity task force setting a target for half of senior leaders to come from working-class or nonprofessional backgrounds by 2030. People from lower socioeconomic backgrounds face numerous barriers to starting a career in finance, according to Ellis. Many opportunities are advertised online and through professional networks that people from lower-income homes are less likely to be in, he says. “First of all, you have to know that the opportunity is there. Then you’ve got to have the confidence to put yourself forward.” One of the challenges businesses face is a lack of data. Unlike gender pay gaps, there is no socioeconomic background reporting requirement for U.K. employers, and only 15% ask about the class background of employees’ parents, according to the Sutton Trust. In 2024, Standard Chartered started polling employees on their backgrounds to get a better understanding of socioeconomic diversity within the business. Disclosure has risen from just under 25% at the start of this year to 31%, with a target to reach 50% by 2028. External consultants have helped redesign how the bank encourages people to disclose, simplifying the process and building trust that the information won’t be used against them. Ellis and other senior leaders have shared their own backgrounds on the bank’s intranet and blog to make others feel more comfortable discussing their upbringing. “If you try to create maximum impact to start with, without understanding the data or creating awareness, you run the risk of not achieving a great deal,” Ellis says. The group also runs quarterly listening sessions where staff talk about the barriers they face. These sessions have helped Ellis develop a better understanding of the challenges others have overcome. “We have unbelievably talented people who have done incredible things to get where they’ve gotten to,” he says. “But they still don’t feel like they necessarily have the confidence or the opportunities that others do.” Those sessions led directly to a talent progression program, launched in June 2026, for high-potential employees from lower-income backgrounds. Each of the 10 participants has been paired with a senior managing director as their sponsor, to give them advocacy, visibility, and access to roles they might otherwise miss. The bank is also working further up the pipeline. Its spring insights program, a three-day, in-person workshop, allows first-year university students to gain experience working in Standard Chartered’s corporate and investment bank. Last year it brought 50 students from underrepresented backgrounds to its offices in the U.K. and the U.S., and 10 of them went on to summer internships. Standard Chartered has also partnered with HSBC to run a schools outreach scheme, which will start in the first quarter of 2027. The initiative aims to demystify finance and show students role models from different backgrounds. “Naturally [HSBC and Standard Chartered] are competitors,” Ellis says. “But on this, the competition goes out the window. Working together creates many more opportunities for young people.” Standard Chartered also works with Progress Together, the industry body founded in 2022. For the first time, three of the 10 program participants will join its cross-company sponsorship scheme, each sponsored by a senior leader at another firm. “I’m more than happy to have the conversation with anyone who wants to work with us that will help advance the agenda,” Ellis says. Cultural and societal differences can also represent a challenge for global businesses when trying to assess socioeconomic diversity. In the U.K. and the U.S., gathering data on employees’ parents’ jobs when they were children is regularly used as an indicator of socioeconomic background. However, in India there is less of a link. “We can’t take what we’ve done in the U.K. and the U.S. and use that in India,” Ellis says. The bank is being guided by its Indian workforce in the design of its local program. Buy-in from senior leadership is also important. “If you want to make any sort of meaningful progress then you need strong sponsorship on the ground,” Ellis says. “Senior leaders play a critical role in driving visibility, building momentum, and ultimately demonstrating that socioeconomic mobility matters, and it matters to the organization.” Alex Manson, CEO of Standard Chartered’s innovation arm, SC Ventures, and Paul Day, group chief internal auditor, became joint global sponsors for the bank’s social mobility program last year. For other companies seeking to improve socioeconomic mobility, Ellis advises collecting data and improving awareness first before introducing new schemes. Token efforts, he warns, can leave employees from lower socioeconomic backgrounds feeling more shut out than before. Nvidia’s secret to retaining staff? No org charts, a flat structure, and two extra days off every quarter
2026-10-08

Nvidia’s secret to retaining staff? No org charts, a flat structure, and two extra days off every quarter

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Much has changed at Nvidia in the 19 years since HR director Febe Punsalan started working at the Santa Clara, Calif.–headquartered company. Two decades ago, Nvidia was largely known for manufacturing the GPUs that allowed computers to render 3D graphics in video games.  Today, it’s a key player in the global AI industry, developing the computer chips that provide the processing power required to train the most powerful models. As employees at frontier AI firms blow the whistle on the supposed existential risks of artificial intelligence, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has proved much more measured in his outlook, putting the chances of AI ending the world at “zero.” Despite the seemingly ever-higher stakes of the AI transition, Nvidia has “always stayed true to our culture,” says Punsalan. “We’re growing in terms of headcount, and we use AI coding and agentic tools every day.” One region where the company is quickly growing is Europe. Nvidia ranks No. 14 on the Fortune 100 Best Companies to Work For–Europe list, and its architecture powers 90% of the continent’s AI factories; the largest GPU cluster in Europe will soon go live in the U.K. “Europe is hugely important to our business, and we’re growing across all regions and functions,” says Punsalan. The company has offices in 15 European countries, and the continent is a critical hub for Nvidia’s robotics and automotive research. Meanwhile, Nvidia has recently hired a head of well-being for EMEA, to adapt perks for local customs. But whether it’s frontier R&D, HR, sales, or engineering, Punsalan says, Nvidia’s guiding principle is that “the mission is always the boss.” This mantra helps guide all employees around a core objective, rather than directing staff through top-down initiatives. Everyone is encouraged to use their voice, Punsalan notes, and the prioritization of tasks begins by listening to employees. “We start by listening. That’s really embedded at Nvidia,” she says. “We’ve got a very flat structure, with no org charts. We live by understanding what the mission is, what we need to drive towards, and then we go for it. I believe that’s what makes Nvidia a great place to work.” This also allows Nvidia to have a flatter organizational structure than other companies of a similar size. People leading strategically important projects will often report straight to the CEO. Huang has stated that he tries to keep the number of layers at Nvidia as low as possible, and as a result, he currently has 36 direct reports. Employees are encouraged to speak up, and Nvidia maintains that it does not prioritize org charts or chains of command. “We care and support our employees throughout the different stages of their lives so they can do their best work at Nvidia.” Money also provides a big incentive. Nvidia, which has a $5.65 trillion market cap, allows workers to buy shares in the company using their salary at a 15% market discount. This benefit has made multimillionaires of its employees. However, some staff have alleged that this can come with the expectation of working late nights and a persistent “pressure cooker” environment. “When you come to work for Nvidia, it’s a challenging place, and it’s challenging work,” acknowledges Punsalan. “But it’s meaningful work, and that resonates whether it’s the U.S., EMEA, APAC, or India. That’s going to come with a lot of dedication.” Despite the high expectations, employee turnover remains low. The global turnover rate at Nvidia is 3.7% compared with the technology industry average of 21.9%, and in the U.K., 95% of Nvidia employees say it’s a great place to work, compared with a 54% average for U.K.-based organizations. That flat organizational structure is an important aspect of Nvidia’s workplace culture. Employees are encouraged to share suggestions with leadership, irrespective of how long they’ve been with the company or their seniority.  Everyone from interns to junior employees to seasoned engineers can bring up ideas through a suggestion box. These are all considered by program managers and, at the very least, discussed in a one-on-one dialogue. “We built that a long time ago, with the notion that every idea is a great idea, and so we’ll look into them,” says Punsalan. “It’s not a ticketing system like you have in other big corporations; every suggestion is looked at and reviewed and has a response.” This initiative led to a company policy of “free days” during the COVID-19 pandemic, which continues today. Each quarter, Nvidia employees globally are given two additional days off. “The entire company shuts down,” says Punsalan. “When you come back to work, you’re not returning with a full inbox because you took a vacation and everyone else was working.  You’re not expected to work at all. That came from employee feedback, from listening, and through ideas.” Nvidia’s workplace policies were recently criticized for being “frugal” compared with those of some competitors because it doesn’t offer staff free lunch and snacks. But Punsalan asserts that the business is focused on other, more important priorities over cafeteria or campus perks such as gym access. (These perks have also been criticized as a way to keep workers on-site.) Instead, Nvidia focuses on improving work-life balance and has maintained a hybrid work program, without strict return-to-office mandates. Nvidia also aims to provide lifelong support for employees. Along with offering paternity and maternity leave, the company helps pay off student loans; provides support for aging parents; reimburses IVF and surrogacy expenses; and offers support programs for neurodiverse employees and parents of neurodiverse children. “Our focus is really on what’s valued by employees,” she says. “We do that through listening—it’s not so much about the food.” Many workers might not experience a roller-coaster trajectory as dramatic as Nvidia’s path from PC graphics provider to AI titan, but every period brings its changes and challenges. “We care and support our employees throughout the different stages of their lives,” adds Punsalan. “And by doing so, they can do their life’s best work at Nvidia.” For the latest insights into the companies on the Fortune 100 Best Companies to Work For–Europe list, visit this page. Europeans are drinking less beer. Heineken’s Europe boss has a plan to avoid a brewing hangover
2026-10-08

Europeans are drinking less beer. Heineken’s Europe boss has a plan to avoid a brewing hangover

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
This summer was a bright moment for Europe’s brewers. Hot weather and the FIFA World Cup packed out beer gardens, and in Britain, the night that England beat Panama was pubs’ busiest of the year, with card transactions five times the daily average, according to Barclays. But behind the sunny terraces, a longer slump has been brewing. European consumers drink less beer than they did before the pandemic. Consumption fell 3.2% in 2025 and is down 9.2% since 2019, according to a report by the industry trade body Brewers of Europe. The slump has been pinned on sober-curious Gen Z drinkers and, more recently, weight-loss drugs like Ozempic. Glenn Caton, Europe president at Heineken, the world’s second-largest brewer, points to costs. “There’s no question that price inflation has impacted consumer consumption over time,” he told Fortune. Brewers had barely recovered from the pandemic when Russia’s 2022 invasion of Ukraine sent energy and grain prices soaring. “Clearly raising prices has been necessary,” Caton said. Heineken raised what it charges British pubs for draught beer by 2.97% in February 2025 and another 2.7% this February. The average U.K. pint rose 17p to £5.34 in the year to April, according to The Morning Advertiser. Although Heineken returned to growth this year, volumes rose 1.6% in the second quarter of 2026, this was not thanks to European drinkers. While beer sales in Asia Pacific and Africa and the Middle East rose, in Europe they’ve been falling. Across Europe and the U.S., Caton said, beer is in “structural decline.” Europe is Heineken’s biggest region by sales but has its thinnest margins. Europe brought in nearly two-fifths (38.4%) of first-half sales but only a quarter of operating profit. Having already raised prices in the region, Caton is now focusing on cutting costs. In February, Heineken announced plans to cut up to 6,000 jobs globally over the next two years under EverGreen 2030, a strategy that aims to save the company up to €500 million a year ($559 million) and boost profits by focusing on its fastest-growing markets. The savings, Caton says, are also meant to offset rising energy and raw-material costs so the company can “mitigate price inflation for the consumer.” About 3,000 jobs were cut in the first half of 2026, and chief financial officer Harold van den Broek has signaled that Europe will bear much of the burden. “Europe is a big component” of the cuts, he said at the half-year results in August. Caton declined to say how many European jobs will go, citing ongoing consultation with staff and the European Works Council. Some of the changes will see roles move to Heineken’s business services hubs in places such as Kraków and India. “You never like to make difficult decisions that impact people you know and respect,” Caton said, “but it is necessary for the health of the company.” Heineken is also merging nine country businesses into four multi-country operating units in Europe, including one bringing together Belgium and the Netherlands and another for Germany, Austria, and Switzerland. Caton says this is a departure from Heineken’s traditionally local-first way of operating. The aim, he said, is a business that is “lean, fast and agile,” with costs low enough to offer drinkers better value. The overhaul also ran for nine months without a permanent chief executive. Rafael Oliveira took over on Oct. 1, but Caton insists the plan never slowed and that the new boss is there to help execute. “It’s not about a radically new strategy,” he said. “My job is to run Europe, and hopefully he’ll let me get on with that.” To keep drinkers who trade down, Heineken sells at every price point, from premium Birra Moretti to mainstream Cruzcampo and budget Foster’s. It is also betting on alcohol-free beer, which lets drinkers cut back without leaving its brands. Zero-alcohol beers, Caton said, is “enabling more consumers to moderate the decline in alcohol.” Before Heineken 0.0 launched in 2017, the category’s big problem “was taste,” he added. Heineken’s version, designed to taste “near identical to the original” and backed by a Formula 1 sponsorship, is now one of the leading alcohol-free beers. The group’s low- and no-alcohol volumes rose 12% in the first half of 2026. Competition in the alcohol-free beer market is growing. “It makes our job harder,” Caton added, “but for the category overall, it’s a good thing, because it’s growing the whole pie.” Alcohol-free accounts for one in 12 beers drunk in the EU, according to Brewers of Europe, and Carton admitted that take-up is uneven across the continent. As a result Heineken is innovating “in both directions,” with flavored 0.0s such as Lemon & Elderflower in the U.K., an orange Cruzcampo, and lagers of around 3.5% alcohol, below the usual 4.5% to 5%. Pubs and bars make up about half of Heineken’s European business, according to Caton. In the U.K., it owns about 2,350 pubs through Star Pubs & Bars, mostly leased to independent operators, and is reportedly in talks to acquire Stonegate as part of a £300 million ($396 million) deal, which would add around 300 more pubs to its portfolio. “Good pubs in the U.K. are thriving,” Caton said. “People still want to socialize physically.” The numbers only partly back him up. British pubs are struggling, with 161 shutting in the first quarter of 2026, up 26% on a year earlier. But the British Beer & Pub Association blames tax and overheads, and across the EU, the share of beer drunk in pubs, bars, and restaurants has stopped shrinking after years of decline. Heineken is also making the case that bars are a key part of the economy. Tourists spend almost €2.5 billion a year on its beers, it estimates, mostly in pubs and bars, and 35% of tourists in a survey the brewer commissioned said they had visited a neighborhood specifically because of a local bar. For Caton, returning Europe to growth will be reliant on creating more value for consumers by reducing the price of drinks in both shops and bars and creating new low-alcohol options for health-conscious consumers. “If you offer great value and a great experience, you bring punters in the door,” he said. And he isn’t worried about beer itself. “It’s been around 5,000 years. It’s not going anywhere.” Sam Birchall is a features writer at Fortune 500 C-Suite Europe. How Siemens Healthineers is helping its international staff feel at home
2026-10-08

How Siemens Healthineers is helping its international staff feel at home

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Relocating for work can bring a lot of additional stress. Alongside learning the requirements of a new role and meeting new colleagues, those moving internationally must also adapt to a new culture and potentially learn a new language. It’s a challenge many people now face. Some 12.9 million people from outside the EU are employed in the EU labor market, according to data from the European Commission. However, expats are not always made to feel welcome. European countries were consistently ranked among the worst countries for expats, according to InterNations’ Expat Insider 2026 report, with many of those surveyed reporting difficulty making friends and feeling at home. In Germany, which came second to last overall, they also struggled with the practicalities of daily life, bureaucracy, housing, and the language. Siemens Healthineers is a medical technology company headquartered in Erlangen, Germany, and employs 74,000 people worldwide. Some of the challenges facing foreign staff began surfacing during its monthly staff surveys with international employees, which represent about one in 10 of its more than 17,000 workers in Germany, reporting that they did not always feel fully integrated. In January 2026, the company launched Beyond Borders, an employee network where international staff can help one another navigate the hurdles of settling in, such as finding a doctor, navigating an insurance policy document, or arranging childcare. Within nine months, the employee resource group had 200 members. Liza Follert, head of people attraction for Europe, the Middle East, and Africa at Siemens Healthineers, says Beyond Borders was created in response to employee feedback. For eight years, the company has sent out a regular staff survey asking them how they are feeling. An anonymized self-identification option, added three years ago, lets the company see which groups are dissatisfied: “80% agree. But who are the 20%?” Follert says. “Knowing the answer tells the company where to act.” Siemens Healthineers ranks No. 9 on the Fortune 100 Best Companies to Work For–Europe list. Follert credits monthly check-ins and the direct impact employees can have in shaping company culture for its ranking in the top 10. Beyond Borders is one of several employee resource groups the company is investing in. Each group is given HR support, a presence on internal channels, an executive sponsor, and a seat in strategy meetings. Other networks have been established for working fathers, LGBTQ+ employees, and early-career staff, to give workers a greater say in their career development as well as opportunities to communicate with leadership. EmpowerME, which supports employees living with cancer, brings staff and patients together. Mental health challenges remain a “huge issue,” according to Follert. Expectations around workload and external factors, including international conflicts and crises, are contributing to these heightened anxieties. Follert adds: “You cannot predict the next oil price rise or the next geopolitical crisis.” In response, the company began training employees in mental health first aid. In some markets, including the U.K. and the U.S., employees also have access to Headspace, a meditation app. Stress scores in the survey have since leveled out, though Follert concedes it is hard to say how much of that is down to the program. Siemens Healthineers once considered training employees as formal brand ambassadors, Follert says, but dropped the idea once it noticed that staff were already doing the job. “Employees write on LinkedIn not just about their work, but about their personal growth, the hard times they have been through, and how the company supported them. Mental health comes up often, as do the pressures of working life as a parent, a woman, an expat, or someone at the start or end of a career.” The supportive employee culture is having a positive impact on staff retention and attraction. Resignations fell by about a quarter in 2025 to 2,336, roughly 3% of employees. Follert has also seen an increase in job applications over the past two years, and more candidates are arriving on a colleague’s recommendation, she says. In Europe, the Middle East, and Africa, employee referrals have risen 13% since 2024. The higher number of applicants and referrals brings its own challenges. “At that volume, the risk is that recruiters become administrators, moving candidates left and right,” Follert says, and she is conscious of the need to create a positive first experience for all candidates. Siemens Healthineers is currently switching to a new recruiting system, which involves moving roughly 2,500 open positions and around half a million candidates onto the new platform. The system works in multiple languages and uses AI to help applicants upload their CVs and find roles that match their skills, while giving recruiters a clearer view of the application process. This transition to a new applicant system is part of a wider change. Siemens Healthineers is currently mid spinoff from parent company Siemens. A vote on the direct spinoff of Siemens Healthineers shares is scheduled to be held at Siemens’s next annual general meeting in February 2027. Follert says that employees have stayed loyal during this transition because the company remained “consistent in the more critical times.” Completing the spinoff, amid economic and geopolitical upheaval, will be the clearest test yet of whether that holds. How might artificial intelligence impact the graduate experience?
2026-10-08

How might artificial intelligence impact the graduate experience?

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Career strategist and talent consultant Elizabeth Lenihan discusses how AI skills and knowledge can amplify the graduate experience, but only when implemented correctly. The STEM graduate experience has changed significantly in response to a sector that is constantly evolving. The expectations, workload, tools and even the overall outcome are not the same as they were before the introduction of advanced technologies such as AI, or even pre-pandemic where work was not as ‘online’ as it is now. Elizabeth Lenihan, a career strategist and talent consultant, explained that currently Ireland’s graduate landscape has narrowed and is more selective than before, for two primary reasons. The first being that Ireland’s economy is concentrated in knowledge-intensive sectors like tech, science and financial services, which makes the labour market more exposed to AI than most advanced economies. She finds that the second factor is behavioural, in that AI has made it easier to disregard dozens of applications in a single afternoon. Lenihan recounts a situation where she had more than 300 applications land across her desk within 24 hours of posting a role, only to find that a significant portion are unaligned with the advertised position. For those applying for a graduate role, who genuinely want to have an edge over the competition, she suggests avoiding batch applying and instead becoming more selective, so you can show specifically what you bring and why you would be an asset. Lenihan told SiliconRepublic.com: “AI hasn’t closed the door on graduate roles, but it is reshaping them. Some of the repetitive groundwork graduates used to learn now runs on its own, so what’s left expects more judgement from day one. “What’s being asked for now is evidence of potential. Can you think, adapt, solve a real problem and work alongside the tools your employer already uses, AI included? That doesn’t mean becoming a specialist overnight, it means showing you can work with it rather than be replaced by the parts of your role it can already do.” So, should students and graduates looking for their next opportunity prioritise a modernised education by developing AI skills? Lenihan thinks they should focus on which layer of the AI economy they are hoping to access: a technical, credentialed role that’s harder to enter but more durable, or a lower-barrier entry point that’s easier to land and likely more exposed. Unlocking the potential of biology for patients Always at the cutting edge of new innovation “As AI keeps evolving, the ability to navigate change, curiosity, communication and judgement will matter just as much as the credential, so build both together rather than betting on one,” she said. But don’t fall into the common trap of reducing your experiences to run-of-the-mill or cliched sentences, despite their popularity. Lenihan explained: “Evidence beats description; a project or a piece of real experience says more than another line claiming ‘strong communication skills’. Don’t underestimate what volunteering, part-time work, education or sport gives you – real stories to draw on instead of speaking hypothetically. “Reflect on the capabilities developed, technical competence, critical thinking, meeting tight deadlines, teamwork and performing under pressure. These are all transferable. Be able to describe the brief, your contribution, the skills applied, and the outcome.” For Lenihan it is important that graduates make use of all available resources and that their journey towards a programme or career is self-driven. She explained: “University career services are underused – most students only walk in during final year, by which point the useful relationships, alumni contacts, and mentors needed a year’s head start. “Beyond that, map out your own motivators and strengths before you start applying, then build relationships, put yourself in front of people, tech meetups, industry events, job fairs and LinkedIn used for real conversations rather than cold applications. I’ve met so many talented graduates at tech events and a memorable personal encounter trumps a name on a page every time.” She added: “You are your career strategy, no one else. Not everyone gets what they want immediately and that is not a failure of the process. The value is in identifying areas of genuine interest and curiosity and then building the skills and experience to move toward them with intention.” She finds that asking clear, focused questions, actively seeking feedback and consistently revising your goals and the level of progress you have made, are powerful practices that can be put into place early on in a career, setting the foundation for learning and longevity. “When that foundation is in place, an interview becomes a conversation rather than an interrogation. Rejection becomes a data point rather than a verdict and difficult feedback becomes information rather than a threat.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
Growing professionally and building teams in the AI age
2026-10-08

Growing professionally and building teams in the AI age

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Larry, a senior vice-president of analytics at Optum, discusses his role in the artificial intelligence space and addressing problems in the healthcare industry. “I’ve worked with Optum, part of UnitedHealth Group, for more than 20 years in a range of roles across data and analytics, spanning various business units,” said Larry, who is currently a senior vice-president of analytics. Throughout his career, he has focused on a number of areas, such as learning core business processes, the financial levers that impact performance and the wider dynamics of the US healthcare market. He told SiliconRepublic.com, “That grounding in business and industry knowledge, paired with an understanding of how analytics can influence and drive strategy and day-to-day execution, opened up broader opportunities along the way. “This is what’s allowed me to progress to leading a global team of more than 700 people focused on some of the hardest problems in healthcare, making the US healthcare system work better.” My role is focused on setting the analytics strategy that supports business priorities across multiple business units, evaluating which technologies to invest in and developing the talent to deliver on both. Alongside that, I work with my leadership team to identify where analytics can drive incremental value, both from a commercial revenue-growth perspective and through an internal cost and productivity lens. Year to year, that means making sure the capability, the talent and the platforms are in place to meet the needs of the business and working with our technology partners to look ahead at where the technology is going, so our strategy is aligned to where capabilities are heading, not where they have been. Day to day, it’s working with key stakeholders to prioritise initiatives and adjust as the business shifts. As an enterprise, we are currently embracing an AI paradigm across our businesses, and scaling AI is critical to our future success. Getting there depends on two things. First: not simply using AI to automate existing processes, but reimagining what those processes could be given what advanced technologies can now do. Second: applying the right AI capability to the right problem. That means resisting the temptation to throw the latest frontier model at everything; often, a traditional machine learning model is better suited and considerably more cost-effective. Key to all of this is trusted data and responsible governance. In healthcare, we have to earn the right to scale AI, protecting privacy, being clear about where human judgement remains essential, and monitoring for unintended bias or variation in outcomes. First, testing and maintaining accuracy, building the evaluation discipline to know whether a model is performing and keeping it performing over time. Second, legal and compliance review, which in healthcare is rightly rigorous and needs to be designed into the process rather than as an afterthought. Third, stakeholder adoption – technology is often the easy part, changing how people work is the hard part. Fourth, integration into existing workflows and systems is oftentimes the longest pole in the tent. And finally, proving value consistently. A technically successful pilot is not enough. We need to measure adoption, operational impact and ultimately, whether it improves outcomes for the people and organisations we serve. Give people headspace to innovate and to keep developing their skills with the latest technologies and approaches. Provide standard frameworks and playbooks for testing and scaling, so teams aren’t reinventing the path to production every time. And put the support structures around them to manage that at scale that include clear routes through compliance, review and deployment. Leaders also need to create psychological safety; teams must be able to surface risks, challenge assumptions and stop or redesign a solution when the evidence says they should. Across teams, it starts with understanding the business and the customer need, then designing something that addresses it. That takes a mix of people – some who know the business, some who know the technology and an operating model that cleanly connects the two. You also need strong data skills, with a deep understanding of the data, where it came from and how good it is, as well as data governance and data engineering as critical skills for any new AI project. For my role, it is about making sure those resources are available and that the connections are being made. It’s also about asking the challenging questions – are we solving the right problem, and is this the right capability and resources to solve it with? The biggest shift has been agentic AI moving from demo to production, and AI evolving from a productivity tool into a core component of the operating model through AI-enabled workflows, decision support and end-to-end process orchestration. Twelve months ago, the discussion centred on what these technologies might do. Today, the focus is on what they have delivered, how we measure and govern performance, and where we scale next. For Optum, this has opened a broader conversation about becoming AI-native across our business processes. The opportunity is no longer simply automating individual tasks, but redesigning how work gets done – simplifying operations, improving affordability, increasing speed and quality, and ultimately delivering a better healthcare experience for patients and care providers. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Sebastian Weidt and team. Image: Universal Quantum The funding will help the business further expand into Singapore, the US, Japan and Germany. University of Sussex-founded Universal Quantum has raised more than $100m in what it said is the largest Series A ever raised by a UK-headquartered quantum company. The round was co-led by Silicon Valley deep-tech investor DCVC and London-based early-stage quantum investor Firgun Ventures, with participation from the likes of Singapore’s EDBI, Artal and IAG Capital. Roblox founder and CEO Dave Baszucki also took part in the round. Universal Quantum was founded in 2018 by long-standing collaborators and Sussex quantum professors Sebastian Weidt and Winfried Hensinger. A year prior in 2017, the two co-published what they called the “first practical blueprint” for large-scale trapped ion quantum computers, which they used as the basis for their start-up. The company develops next-generation quantum devices for clients including the German Aerospace Centre. Its computers are designed by linking together many advanced quantum chips to deliver the overall high-quality qubit count required to solve problems that classical computers generally cannot, according to the company. “Universal Quantum’s progress reflects our partners’ belief that we represent one of the most credible paths to quantum computing at scale,” said Weidt, the company’s CEO. “This round unites an extraordinary group of investors from the world’s most important pools of capital around a single mission: delivering utility-scale quantum computing.” The new capital will be used to further develop and commercialise Universal’s products, the company said, as well as supporting a recently announced expansion into Singapore – where it has established its first R&D centre outside Europe – as well as in the US, Japan and across its German hub in Hamburg. “Having laid some of the earliest foundations for large-scale trapped-ion architectures, Sebastian and Winfried are true pioneers in the field,” commented Zeynep Korutürk, founding and managing partner of Firgun Ventures. “With Europe under pressure to prove it can compete on frontier technologies, we are proud to invest in a European company that has grown into a global player, one that is well positioned to help define the future of industrial-scale quantum computing.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
Pioneering molecular reaction duo win Nobel Prize in Chemistry
2026-10-08

Pioneering molecular reaction duo win Nobel Prize in Chemistry

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
From left: Henri Kagan and Kenso Soai. Image: Illustrated by Niklas Elmehed © Nobel Prize Outreach Henri Kagan and Kenso Soai were recognised for the discovery of non-linear effects and autocatalysis in asymmetric organic synthesis. Two scientists from France and Japan have won the Nobel Prize in Chemistry for their discoveries that proved to be crucial to how chemists design reactions for the manufacture of pharmaceuticals, flavours, scents and new materials. Henri Kagan and Kenso Soai were recognised by the Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences (RSAS) for the discovery of non-linear effects and autocatalysis in asymmetric organic synthesis. The pair’s research is considered foundational to understanding the principles of ‘homochirality’, part of a phenomenon whereby some molecules, such as amino acids, exist as two variants that are each other’s mirror image, but living organisms will only contain one of these images. The Nobel laureates’ work laid out a path for how to design and initiate certain types of chemical reactions so that greater quantities of a favourable molecule are selectively created at the expense of its naturally occurring mirror image that might be wasteful or harmful in an intended use context. Kagan is credited with discovering, in the 1980s, a revolutionary new way of manipulating chemical reactions to allow for a greater excess of one of the molecular mirror images to be created than was previously assumed possible. In 2003, Soai designed and executed the first chemical reaction in which only one of the potential mirror images was formed. The ‘Soai reaction’ is considered “one of the most spectacular chemical experiments ever conducted”, according to RSAS. “Henri Kagan and Kenso Soai have provided a solution to a chemical mystery that is over a century old: how homochirality can emerge spontaneously,” said Heiner Linke, chair of the Nobel Committee for Chemistry. “The chemical reactions they have developed are spectacular.” According to RSAS, understanding reaction mechanisms is a fundamental part of organic chemistry, with the rationale that emerges from such activities useful for prediction, control, and design of new and as-yet untested reactions that can address unsolved problems. “The discoveries awarded with the 2026 Nobel Prize in Chemistry have deepened our appreciation of molecular chirality, with implications that extend well beyond the realms of organic chemistry,” said Peter Somfai, a professor of organic chemistry at Lund University and a member of the awarding committee. Kagan, born in France in 1930, and Soai, born in Japan in 1950, will share a prize of 12m Swedish kronor (€1.07m). Earlier this week, the 2026 Nobel Prizes in Physiology or Medicine and in Physics were awarded to scientists from the US, Germany and Belgium. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Futurism
It Turns Out That It’s Pretty Easy to Get PlayStation 5 Games to Run on PC
2026-10-08

It Turns Out That It’s Pretty Easy to Get PlayStation 5 Games to Run on PC

Futurism | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
For decades, gaming console emulators have allowed tech-savvy users to play their favorite games on officially unsupported devices. While the trend has broadly been confined to older-generation consoles like the Nintendo 64 or GameBoy, hackers are now starting to get even games exclusive to current-generation hardware, like Sony’s PlayStation 5, to run on a PC. Thanks to rapid advancements in emulator tech, PS5 titles are becoming increasingly playable on virtually any modern PC, as Ars Technica reports. Videos making the rounds on social media show PS5 games, like “Dead Cells,” comfortably running “fully natively” on a PC thanks to a new project called AnyPS5, which has been making major strides. While it’s presumably only a matter of time until Sony will come after the project and try to shut it down, the stakes are considerable. We’re just weeks away from the release of “Grand Theft Auto VI,” likely the most anticipated video game launch of all time, which will come out on current-gen consoles only. Developer Rockstar has yet to reveal an official PC release date for the game. Users on social media are celebrating AnyPS5’s recent progress, highlighting gamers’ already strained relationship with Sony, a company that has lost much of its goodwill with longtime fans. Sony reiterated earlier this year that digital games are “licensed” and not “sold,” effectively telling its customers that they don’t actually own their games. The company also announced that it would completely stop producing physical PS game discs in 2028, angering many in the gaming community. “Sony just needs to start getting used to not owning its console,” one Reddit user wrote in response to the latest AnyPS5 progress news. “This project is like a piracy moonshot for the GTA 6 launch. And i think they might just about make it,” wrote another. While some emulators, like SharpEmu and KytyPS5, take the traditional route of using homebrewed software to coax software into running on an unsupported system, AnyPS5 is taking a different approach. Instead of acting like a traditional emulator, AnyPS5 translates a PS5 title’s game code into instructions that can be read by any computer using the outdated but once ubiquitous 32-bit processor architecture x86. As TweakTown reports, AnyPS5 now supports roughly 96 percent of its tracked PS5 GPU shader instructions. Put differently, it’s making extremely fast progress towards of the goal of allowing any PS5 game to run “natively” on a PC. Just last week, it was at a mere 50 percent. “By avoiding emulation altogether, AnyPS5 can potentially eliminate some of the computational overhead, resulting in a smoother experience,” Tom’s Hardware explains. While we still aren’t at the point where any PS5 game can run on a PC, it’s looking like it might only be a matter of time — and that could bode badly for companies betting on the release of GTA VI. At the same time, it could be a major source of hope for gamers who feel like they’ve continuously been taken advantage of by unaffordable hardware and console exclusives who shut out the most ubiquitous gaming platform of all. Updated to remove a reference to Dead Cells being a PS5 exclusive. More on gaming: Microsoft Patents Technique for Inserting Ads Into Videogames While You’re Playing Them

Taylor Swift Applies for Top Level Domain Sites That End in .Taylorswift
2026-10-08

Taylor Swift Applies for Top Level Domain Sites That End in .Taylorswift

Futurism | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
In 2024, award-winning singer songwriter Taylor Swift became the victim of a wave of pornographic deepfakes flooding social media feeds. The controversy set the stage for a pandemic of nonconsensual AI-generated nudes, forcing big tech companies into damage control mode and even striking up a political brawl during the runup to the 2024 presidential elections. Even at the time, Swift’s lawyers were no strangers to their client being the subject of harassment. Almost a decade previously, Swift bought the web addresses TaylorSwift.porn and TaylorSwift.adult right before they went public in 2015, ensuring that nobody could use the domains for any abusive purposes. At the time, the Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN), the international regulatory body and nonprofit that presides over the internet’s databases and generic top-level domains (TLD), like .com, had recently broadened the available list, prompting public figures to jump into action. Now, according to an October 7 ICANN application, Taylor Swift’s TAS Rights Management company, a Tennessee-based IP holding and management company established to protect Swift’s copyrights, brand assets, and trademarks, has applied for the rights to yet another top-level domain: .taylorswift. Thanks to the proliferation of AI agents, setting up a new website — like official.taylorswift, for instance — has also become far more accessible than it was just a few years ago, giving copycats a major leg up. “We intend to operate the .TAYLORSWIFT TLD as a dotBrand TLD where all domain registrations will be restricted to Affiliate organizations (as defined in the Registry Agreement) and, potentially, to licensees of our trademarks,” Swift’s LLC wrote in its application. “We will implement a strict registration approval process to ensure registrations are limited to these types of registrants.” On Wednesday, the ICANN revealed over 1,600 new applications for generic top level domains (gTLDs) so far this year, including 333 brand applications. Before .taylorswift gets officially ratified, the ICANN will open a community input period, which ends in March 2027, with approvals following in the “first half” of next year. Other brand applications include .adidas, .bankofamerica, .tiktok, and .shopify. Meta’s Registry Holdings submitted a whopping 15, including .aiglasses, .instagram, .metaglasses, .muse, and .whatsapp. As part of her efforts to protect her own brand in the age of AI, in April Swift applied to trademark her voice and appearance. At the time, she lodged three applications in the US — currently still pending — including a photo of herself on stage and two audio clips of her introducing herself. Months earlier, Hollywood star Matthew McConaughey filed eight trademark applications, featuring videos and audio clips of him “staring, smiling and talking.” “My team and I want to know that when my voice or likeness is ever used, it’s because I approved and signed off on it,” he told the Wall Street Journal at the time. “We want to create a clear perimeter around ownership with consent and attribution the norm in an AI world.” More on Taylor Swift: Even Longtime Swifties Disgusted by Taylor’s Use of AI

Influencers Are Promoting Cheap Dupes of Meta AI Glasses that Don’t Have the “Annoying Blinking Light” When They Record
2026-10-08

Influencers Are Promoting Cheap Dupes of Meta AI Glasses that Don’t Have the “Annoying Blinking Light” When They Record

Futurism | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
“I’m recording you right now and you probably can’t tell, because there’s no annoying little blinking light on these glasses,” a creator boasting nearly half a million followers declares in an August TikTok video. “I’m gonna be the first one to tell you, go save that $400, and grab some of these from TikTok Shop.” A disclaimer on the video denotes that the creator is “eligible for commission,” meaning that if a follower clicks through a shopping tag included on the video, he may get an affiliate kickback. That shopping tag directs viewers to a TikTok Shop page hawking a range of wireless camera- and AI-equipped glasses. The glasses appear to be on perma-sale, and go for about $40 to $100. It’s just one of many TikTok Shop pages that market far-cheaper knockoffs — or “dupes” — of the tech giant Meta’s AI Glasses. The imitations are sold on TikTok Shop, Amazon, Temu, Walmart, and other marketplaces under a rotating mill of junky brand names, and are expressly promoted by many affiliate commission-seeking creators on TikTok as cheaper alternatives to Meta’s market-leading spectacles, which go for several hundred dollars. But the price difference isn’t the only feature that some creators point to as a reason to pick up the knockoffs. In some cases, according to influencer videos we reviewed, a key part of the pitch is that unlike Meta’s smart glasses, the cheaper spectacles allegedly don’t feature a recording light designed to signal to those around a wearer that they’re being recorded. “I’m recording you right now and you probably can’t even tell, because these don’t have that annoying blinking light,” another influencer says in a video from March in a strikingly similar message. “Coming from someone with the Meta Bans and the TikTok Shop version, I’ma just say, get the TikTok Shop version, because you’re gonna be saving your money, and they do the same thing.” That cheaper dupes for the Meta glasses could be used to discreetly record others in their most private moments has even been used as a marketing hook in promotional content shared by influencers. One couple, a man and a woman whose TikTok account is dedicated to shopping content designed to garner affiliate commissions on TikTok Shop purchases, have made several videos promoting less-expensive smart glasses. In some of these videos, they market what they claim are light-free glasses by pretending that the woman is unaware that her partner was recording her during sex due to the lack of an indicator light. “Whew! Dang, baby. That was real good — and a good video, too,” the man tells the woman in one such video. The woman, who’s filmed point-of-view-style as she pretends to zip up her pants, feigns confusion. “Video?” she asks. “Oh, you didn’t know?” the man responds. The woman, still looking confused, notes that “there’s no flash.” “Baby, these aren’t those $400 ones, okay,” the man shoots back, before launching into a rundown of the cheaper recording shades’ features. Most of the cheaper brands being promoted in the videos we reviewed appear to be connected to the same app: HeyCyan, an AI service that promises to make “smart glasses truly smart.” The HeyCyan app is developed by China-based company called Shenzhen Qingcheng, and is currently being investigated by the Australian government, which says it’s seeking clarity over the company’s privacy practices. We sent messages to several TikTok Shop sellers linked to by creators. Only one brand, LUSBY, responded. It told us — using an email address linked to a website for a leaf blower, for some reason — that it “does not currently operate an official TikTok Shop” and that “the TikTok accounts you shared are not affiliated with us, and we have not authorized or paid these creators to promote our products.” “Unfortunately, we have encountered unauthorized sellers and accounts using our brand name and promotional content without our permission. This is an issue we take seriously,” the smart-glasses-and-leaf-blower venture continued. It also insisted that its glasses “do have a recording indicator light.” “We do not promote our products as devices designed for recording people without their knowledge, and we do not endorse misleading claims about their features,” the company added. To that end, some creators’ claims about the lack of indicator lights are questionable. Some of the featured glasses very much appear to be outfitted with what looks like an LED light, and in some videos, commenters claiming to have purchased the same pair have clamored that the lights are there and functional. In other videos, rather than insinuating that the indicator light is nonfunctional, creators have marketed knockoffs by hyping up the fact that the LED lights can easily be blocked by inexpensive stickers. “If I’m having a conversation with you in public bro, you would not know I’m recording you bro,” one creator shares in an “eligible for commission” video from August. “I’m recording you now but you can’t see that, because I got that light-blocker right here.” In the comments of one video we reviewed, a creator claimed that her glasses even came with a cover-up sticker to block the indicator light. “I’m recording you and you didn’t even know it,” a different creator says in a video posted in April, in yet another extremely similar worded message. “I’m also recording you to show you that there’s really no difference between the TikTok AI glasses and the Metas.” Asked in a comment if “the lights come on while recording,” the creator wrote back that “they do not,” adding in a follow-up comment that they “come with cover-ups… so the light won’t be seen.” Though tech companies including Google and Snapchat have been trying and failing to popularize smart glasses since the 2010s, Meta’s smart glasses, created in partnership with the popular eyewear brands Ray-Ban and Oakley — and soon, the luxury fashion juggernaut Prada — have been the first to achieve significant adoption. But as their popularity has grown, Meta’s smart glasses have increasingly been caught at the center of a privacy firestorm. In addition to concerns about Meta’s handling of sensitive data and revelations about unreleased facial recognition features, many wearers have used the tech to covertly record others in public and even private spaces, giving rise to phrases like “the Ray-Ban Meta creep” and the disparaging moniker “pervert glasses.” Harassing “pickup artists” have recorded themselves approaching women in the street and recording them without their consent, while other influencers have filmed themselves bothering workers at their place of business — footage that is then published online, publicizing the harassment for content’s sake. As Futurism reported in August, middle and high school boys have started using smart glasses to replicate this style of content at schools, sharing videos of them harassing peers — mostly girls — to the point of frustration and violence. In the face of abuse, Meta has moved to kick influencers posting harassing content off its platforms. It also says it’s planning to release a camera-free version of the glasses — though they’ll still record audio — and has made efforts to crack down on widespread tampering with the indicator light, which has become a cottage industry for handy side-hustlers seeking to earn some cash by putting glasses into “stealth mode.” Even so, Meta has pointed to its inclusion of the flashing indicator light as evidence of its commitment to privacy, and is clearly seeking to quash abusive behavior and salvage its glasses’ reputation. Meanwhile, regardless of whether cheap dupes actually include indicator lights — or if, alternatively, theirs are just much easier to damage or block — it’s clear that some companies and creators view the prospect of covert, light-free recordings as a need to be filled in the market, rather than an abusive practice that needs to be curbed. “You didn’t even know I’m recording. Discreet,” yet another creator announces in a promotional video, this time in a March video marketing what he claims are light-free smart glasses. To show proof of the camera’s quality, the creator shares a recording captured at a local gym, with several other gym-goers visible in the shot. “Perfect for traveling, content creating,” the influencer continues, “and people who want to be nosy.” More on Meta glasses: Teen Boys Are Using Meta Glasses to Harass and Bully Girls at High Schools and Middle Schools

Second result: AI lookalike by ‘K. Rose,'” Roose, a former New York Times tech columnist, fumed in a tweet, posting a screenshot of the scam. “Embarrassing!” Long after @kashhill and others flagged this problem, Amazon is still getting gamed by print-on-demand slop publishers. This is what the search results for The AGI Chronicles look like. First result: real book. Second result: AI lookalike by “K. Rose.” Embarrassing! pic.twitter.com/o2Il95yU7X Ironically, the cover of the AI-generated dupe removed some of the mangled title text that was deliberately imitating AI hallucinations in the original. It seems like the scam might be working too. Though the copycat boasts no reviews, Amazon lists it as the “#1 New Release” in the category of “Human-Computer Interaction.” The third best-selling book in this category, according to the website, is the Kindle Edition of Roose’s book. These schemes have long plagued the platform, and despite years of reporting on the issue, little has been done to stop it. OpenAI Says Teens Can Talk About Suicide for Hours Before ChatGPT Will Notify Parents
