OpenAI Says Teens Can Talk About Suicide for Hours Before ChatGPT Will Notify Parents

Futurism | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Content warning: this story includes discussion of self-harm and suicide. If you are in crisis, please call, text or chat with the Suicide and Crisis Lifeline at 988, or contact the Crisis Text Line by texting TALK to 741741. OpenAI has made an eyebrow-raising claim: that teen users can talk to ChatGPT about suicide and other harmful topics for hours before the chatbot notifies their parents. The admission came amid an investigation by Common Sense Media’s Youth AI Safety Institute. In its report released Wednesday, the nonprofit group found that OpenAI’s new ChatGPT for Teens mode, which is designed to place stricter guardrails for adolescent users and comes with built-in parental controls, posed an “unacceptable risk” to children because its putative safety features rarely functioned as marketed. During the tests, the researchers posed as teenagers and explicitly discussed topics like self-harm, suicide, and eating disorders with ChatGPT for Teens. But one of its most crucial features, parental notifications, were only triggered twice during the 450 prompts the researchers tested. Those notifications also weren’t repeated when the conversations continued to discuss the dark topics. “You can talk to ChatGPT as a teenager for up to an hour about suicide, self-harm, or various varying types of eating disorders, and ChatGPT will tell you that it’s not going to tell anybody about your conversations, and you will get no parental notifications,” Robbie Torney, senior director of AI programs at at Common Sense Media, told Futurism in an interview. But by OpenAI’s own admission, these conversations could go on without a parent being notified for even longer. “For one of the features we evaluated — parental notifications — OpenAI disclosed after our tests that its systems can take several hours to activate on newly linked accounts,” Common Sense Media said in a statement. We reached out to OpenAI to ask whether Common Sense Media’s claims were accurate. “The few hour delay is not for each notification; it occurs when the initial request to link the teen and parent accounts happens. When a parent requests to link their account to their teen, we perform several checks to confirm their identity,” a spokesperson told us. “To ensure we do this securely at scale, linking the accounts can take several hours, but it is often much faster.” “Could you imagine if we connected a teen to the wrong parent — or even worse, a bad actor?” the spokesperson continued. “Once their accounts are linked, we send parents a notification if there is perceived risk (eg, suicide, eating disorders, etc.) and we have committed to send those in less than 1 hour.” OpenAI responded to Common Sense Media’s report by calling its methodology flawed, and claimed that the bulk of the testing took place when the parental controls weren’t fully rolled out. Common Sense Media, standing by its finding, hit back by saying it confirmed with OpenAI that the features were fully launched before conducting the tests. The findings are alarming not only because the teen-friendly guardrails appear to be dangerously deficient, but because OpenAI has almost certainly misled parents into thinking its tech was safer than it was before. It’s not unlikely that some parents who were wary about letting their child use a chatbot were brought around by the company’s public show of being serious about teen safety. “This new version of ChatGPT for Teens, isn’t actually acting that differently than the previous version of ChatGPT,” warned Torney. “And that’s not going to be apparent to a parent.” More on OpenAI: OpenAI Publicist Tries to Shut Down Interviewer When Sam Altman Is Asked About Woman Who Killed Herself After Talking to ChatGPT

Finland Orders Google to Suspend New Data Centers After Contractors Pave Over Protected Forests

Futurism | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Finish environmental authorities are in a rage over Google’s $15 billion data center projects after the discovery that company contractors had chopped down some 330 hectares of government-protected forests, seemingly without a permit. According to Agence France-Presse, the Finnish Supervisory Agency (LVV) demanded that the Google-affiliate company Tuike Finland Oy “immediately suspend… all preparatory measures that would significantly alter the environment” in connection with two planned data center developments in the Northern municipalities of Muhos and Kajaani. In all, LVV accuses Google’s contractor of clear-cutting some 330 hectares in Muhos without completing a mandatory environmental impact assessment, a necessary step for receiving approval. The company cleared a further 200 hectares for its data center in Kajaani, though it isn’t clear whether that logging was approved by authorities. As the Guardian reported, Google has just over a week to explain its actions to Finnish officials. Though a government review is only just getting underway, the tech giant is already expressing remorse. In a statement to AFP, Google spokesperson Sondre Ronander admitted the company messed up, saying “we understand the concerns and have fallen short of our own high standards in this instance.” “We will study the LVV’s findings and follow their guidance,” Ronander continued. As head of the environmental department of the LVV Tommi Muilu told AFP, Google has not only felled hundreds of acres of trees, but has stripped topsoil, engineered roads and storage areas, and altered ditches. Speaking to AFP, chair of the long-running Finnish Association for Nature Conservation said Google had paved over “nature sites which should be preserved.” More on data centers: Amazon Warns Local Communities to Stop Defying Its Massive AI Data Centers

A startup founder who served time in prison is looking to court an untapped market: ex-cons

TechCrunch | 2026-10-08

REGISTER NOW. Richard Bronson, an entrepreneur who recently launched a startup called Commissary Club, has an unusual background for a founder: in a previous life, he spent time in prison. In the 1980s, Bronson worked for the notorious Stratton Oakmont — the corruption-riddled brokerage firm that was run by Jordan Belfort, the so-called “Wolf of Wall Street” whose story became a 2013 film. The firm collapsed in the mid-1990s under the weight of misconduct accusations. Bronson was a partner at Stratton, but left after a year to start his own firm in South Florida. He was quite successful in that venture, until he wasn’t. “I built a $100 million business taking these small cap companies public,” Bronson told TechCrunch. “Some of the success, however, came through breaking the law — securities laws — and ultimately, as I knew inevitably would happen, I had to pay the price. I lost everything, and most significantly, my freedom. And I was sent to federal prison for a couple of years.” Bronson has been out of prison for a long time now, but he has never forgotten the struggles he had when he initially emerged from prison. “Nobody would hire me, despite all of my education and work experience and everything else. I was like completely radioactive,” he said. Bronson’s experience after prison helped inform his current startup, an employment-focused social media platform called Commissary Club, which takes its name from the store inside a prison where inmates buy snacks and toiletries. The site is specifically marketed to ex-cons, and uses AI to help them navigate the often confusing process of finding employment and housing after they reenter the world. “I could not help but notice how little assistance was available to people coming out of prison,” said Bronson. “If it was this bad for me, what about the guys I was in prison with who had very little education and very little job experience? What were their chances?” Commissary Club also has a social component, which Bronson said he added because he knows how isolating the post-prison experience can be. “It’s almost like Facebook,” he said, “where people can not only find job, and get the help with the jobs and the housing and access to benefits, but they find the other missing ingredient that’s existentially important to them that isn’t satisfied elsewhere. They find community.” Judging by the site, Commissary Club also appears primed to become a referral network, and it offers a way for users to find dates. For now, Bronson’s startup is a team of two — just him and his co-founder, Roman Kissin, who Bronson called the technical mind (or “the brains”) behind the project. Kissin previously worked for eBay and IBM and, between 2022 and 2025, served as the CTO of LexisNexis, according to his LinkedIn profile. The site is free at first, then switches to paid. Users get “the first 10 applications for free, and then we bundle the next at $149 for 10 applications, or $14.99 each,” Bronson told TechCrunch. Some may balk at that for-profit model, and for people just out of prison, it may be a tough sell. Yet Bronson sees it as a win-win — a business that can help a community that few others are trying to help. “People immediately assume, ‘Oh, is this a nonprofit?’ And the answer is no,” said Bronson. There’s a strong mission component, certainly,” he continued,” and I have devoted my life, when I came out of prison, to helping my brothers and sisters with records. Part of it is because I just felt so remorseful for the shit that I had done. I still wake up every day regretting choices I made. “But we’re going after a huge, huge market that nobody — I mean nobody — does anything about,” said Bronson. “People talk about AI taking away employment and people losing jobs. I look at it differently. We’re building AI that creates employment for people that society left behind.” Bronson’s Commissary Club was selected for this year’s TechCrunch Startup Battlefield 200, as part of TechCrunch’s storied Battlefield Competition; come join us next week in San Francisco to see the competition live. Natura’s $99 smart ring puts AI agents on your finger
2026-10-08

Natura’s $99 smart ring puts AI agents on your finger

TechCrunch | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Meet Interface, a new $99 smart ring from AI and hardware startup Natura that’s designed for the era of AI agents. With a press of your finger, you can ask an AI agent to complete a task, capture thoughts, and get things done without reaching for your phone. And while AI is at the heart of Interface, the ring also doubles as a health tracker, as it monitors metrics like heart rate, HRV, sleep, and activity. The startup was founded by Carlo Edoardo Ferraris, who previously founded tech accessories company Rolling Square. After years of building consumer hardware, he became convinced that rapidly advancing AI needed a new kind of interface that lets people ask for what they want the way they’d ask a person. The idea behind Interface is to help people look up from their phones while getting everyday tasks done more quickly and naturally. At launch, the ring will connect with a range of AI agents and apps, including Meta’s Muse, Instinct, Grokbot, Claude, ChatGPT, and more. Pre-orders are expected to open next month, with shipping slated for December or January. The smart ring was not the company’s first product. Those were HumanPods, a pair of AI-enabled earbuds designed to let users interact with AI agents. But Natura believed it needed a new form factor built for the latest advances in voice technology. “Over time, we evolved to an understanding where you really want to have access to your agents 24/7,” Ferraris told TechCrunch in an interview. “You don’t want to have even one hour of your day where you cannot access them. And the form factor of the ring is perfect for this because it’s basically an extension of you. You can go shower with it; you can sleep with it. You never take it off.” The company says the use cases are endless. If you’re out for a run, you could ask the ring to start tracking your workout. When you wake up, you could ask what’s on your schedule for the day. If you’re in the shower and realize you’re out of shampoo, you could ask the ring to reorder it. If you’re falling asleep while watching TV, you could tell it to turn off the TV and set an alarm for the morning. After using it to record a meeting, you could ask it to send your notes from the discussion to a coworker. Responses from agents can be delivered through connected headphones, an iPhone Live Activity, or the NatureOS app. Plus, users who rely on multiple AI agents and apps can specify which one they want to handle a particular task. For example, they could ask Claude to write code, Instinct to book a reservation, and Grokbot to take care of something else. In addition to controlling AI agents, users can control their devices, including their computers. Ferraris says he believes that, ultimately, most of the things people do on their computers will be handled through AI agents. He sees the ring eventually replacing many tasks that currently require screens, from filling out forms and messaging in apps to finding files and drafting presentations, with simple voice requests to an AI agent. “Ultimately, the main interface between users and technology is going to be the ring,” Ferraris said. “So it’s not going to be immediate because even today, not everybody has a personal agent, but I think everybody is bound to have one. So in this first phase, we expect it’s going to be for the people that already have a personal agent, which is mostly people in tech. But eventually, I think within a year, more or less, most people will have a personal agent.” Although the ring will launch at $99, Natura plans to eventually raise the price. For now, the company wants to get the device into people’s hands, build usage, and create demand before doing so. After an initial three- to six-month free period, Natura plans to charge a $9 monthly subscription fee. The company says the ring’s manufacturing costs are comparable to those of products like the Oura Ring and Samsung Galaxy Ring because they use many of the same components. However, Natura plans to price its ring lower initially to reduce the barrier to entry and drive adoption. As for health tracking, the ring tracks heart rate, resting heart rate, heart rate variability, different sleep stages, daily movement and step count, and skin temperature over time. The company says the ring can last 6 to 12 days on a full charge, depending on usage, and takes 100 minutes to fully recharge. While the smart ring market is filled with health-focused offerings from companies like Oura, Ultrahuman, and RingConn, as well as AI-focused ones from companies like Pebble and Vocci, Ferraris believes Interface stands out by combining both worlds. “This ring that we’re about to launch is significantly different compared to any other ring out there today because we were able to combine not only the highest quality health tracking with the rest of the components that allow for agent communication and meeting recordings and external memory, but we were also able to do that with an incredibly long battery life, which is vital for a product like this, and in a size that is very, very small. The product itself is, from a hardware perspective, very different from anything else that’s out there.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Goodfire says its new ‘inside-out’ monitors catch rogue AI agents at a fraction of the cost
2026-10-08

Goodfire says its new ‘inside-out’ monitors catch rogue AI agents at a fraction of the cost

TechCrunch | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. The standard way to keep an AI agent in line is to have a second AI read over its shoulder. It’s been the default approach, but it can get expensive fast when agents run for hours and process the equivalent of several novels’ worth of text. Goodfire, a startup focused on interpretability (figuring out how AI models work internally), launched a cheaper option on Thursday: monitors that watch what’s happening inside an AI model as it works, rather than just reading what it writes. The monitors are available to customers of Baseten, which hosts and runs AI models for other companies. Baseten’s Base Labs announced a safety partnership with Goodfire and the AI platform Hugging Face last month. The launch comes after a string of incidents this year in which AI agents escaped their test environments, including OpenAI agents that breached Hugging Face. Kimi K3, the open model Goodfire built its first monitor around, took advantage of a leak in its sandbox to access the internet and information on GitHub this summer. Goodfire’s system works a bit like airport security. Small detectors called probes read the model’s internal signals at every step of an agent’s work, the way a walk-through scanner checks every passenger. Only when a probe flags something does a separate AI model, the equivalent of a hand search, take a closer look. Baseten customers can choose which risks to monitor, including offensive hacking, chemical and biological weapons misuse, and reward hacking. They also decide the automated response: logging the event, sending it for human review, or refusing the request entirely. Goodfire says its approach is also cheaper to run. Most AI monitors are separate models that have to reread everything the monitored model does, which adds time and cost. Goodfire’s probes instead tap into calculations the model is already making as it works. “Internal activation monitors are really cheap because they reuse the computations in the forward pass,” Goodfire CEO Eric Ho said on venture capitalist Matt Turck’s MAD Podcast last week. “So the model’s already computing this token. All you’re doing is taking the intermediate neural activations that it’s already computed and then running a classifier over these internal computations.” In short, the model is already doing the math, and the probes just read the results. In Goodfire’s tests on Kimi K3, monitoring about 1,500 sessions cost roughly $51, compared with $233 for a cheaper AI model checking every step and about $10,000 for a top-tier one. The probes caught 94% of malicious hacking sessions and sent 8.7% of harmless ones for a second look. Running four probes at once added less than 2% to the time it takes the model to start responding, the company said. “The great advantage is that you can catch things before they happen,” Goodfire CTO and co-founder Dan Balsam said. “We can detect when the model might hack during eval or training.” The pitch is aimed at open models. Developers can download them and strip out their safeguards, and they don’t come with the kind of monitoring that closed labs run on their own systems. “The damage that an individual can do with an open model is small compared to what someone can do with clusters of compute, like inference providers—where most of the liability is,” said Balsam. “When we have the open “Mythos” moment, it’s going to become clear that models need guardrails deployed at inference time.” Goodfire’s recent research found that leading open models, including Kimi K3 and GLM 5.2, reward-hacked in 50% to 96% of runs on tests of AI agents. Goodfire isn’t the first to try this approach. Google DeepMind said in January that its research informed the deployment of misuse-detection probes in Gemini. Balsam said the monitors are the near-term piece of a longer research goal: reverse-engineering an LLM so that behavior can be traced back to where it emerged in training. “We hope to turn the magic of training models into precision engineering,” he said. Elon Musk questions Ambani’s influence as Starlink India launch stalls
2026-10-08

Elon Musk questions Ambani’s influence as Starlink India launch stalls

TechCrunch | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Elon Musk has questioned the influence of Indian billionaire Mukesh Ambani as SpaceX struggles to launch its Starlink satellite internet service in the country. Musk’s comments, which were posted on X, escalate a dispute over regulatory delays after the Indian government rejected his claims that powerful business interests were blocking its entry. Musk accused on Wednesday unnamed “oligarchs” of blocking Starlink’s launch in India to maintain what he called their “monopolistic chokehold” on the country, without identifying them or providing evidence for his claim. He called the alleged blocking a “crime against the people of India” and argued that Starlink could bring affordable, high-speed internet to people who lack internet access. Hours later, India’s Ministry of Communications pushed back, saying its regulatory framework for satellite communications is “fair and non-discriminatory.” The ministry said in a statement that Starlink and two other licensed satellite operators are all at “broadly the same regulatory stage.” It added that all three companies are undergoing security assessments that must be completed before they can seek spectrum, the radio frequencies that satellites use to send signals. Musk went further on Thursday, questioning the influence of Reliance Industries chairperson Mukesh Ambani. ”Is Ambani the real boss of India?” he wrote on X, after claiming that Starlink had spent years complying with Indian regulations and questioning why it still lacked a license. Starlink has spent more than five years trying to enter India, a market with more than a billion internet subscribers. But it has yet to begin commercial services despite securing key regulatory approvals and partnering with telecom giants Reliance Jio and Bharti Airtel last year. SpaceX has also set up a local team as it prepares for a launch, appointing former Payoneer executive Prabhakar Jayakumar to lead its India operations in December. Musk’s comments came hours after Lauren Dreyer, Starlink’s vice president of business operations, appeared at India Mobile Congress, a telecom industry conference in New Delhi and reiterated the company’s commitment to the market. “We stand ready to serve India,” Dreyer said, adding that Starlink wants to help realize Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s goal of achieving universal connectivity across the country. Starlink said it already has more than 20 gateway sites (the ground stations that link its satellites to the internet) and hundreds of antennas on the ground in India, and has adapted its operations to meet the country’s regulatory, security, and data-sovereignty requirements. Moreover, SpaceX has positioned its satellite network as a complement to — rather than a replacement for — India’s terrestrial telecom networks. SpaceX first attempted to launch Starlink in India in 2021. The company, however, had to refund preorders for its equipment in early 2022 after the Indian government called it out for “booking/rendering the satellite internet service” before getting the necessary licenses. Starlink has also previously clashed with India’s largest telecom operators over how satellite spectrum should be allocated. Starlink pushed for spectrum to be assigned administratively, while Reliance Jio argued that it should be auctioned — a position that could have made Starlink’s entry more expensive. India ultimately sided with Starlink’s preferred approach, deciding to allocate satellite spectrum administratively rather than through an auction. Jio and Airtel, India’s two biggest telcom operators, are also pursuing their own satellite internet services, even as they have agreed to distribute Starlink in India. Jio is working with satellite operator SES on satellite broadband, while Airtel is a major backer of Eutelsat OneWeb, another licensed satellite operator in India. While the timing of Starlink’s India launch remains unclear, the company this week launched Starlink Mobile, which connects ordinary phones to satellites, in neighboring Bangladesh. Starlink, India’s Ministry of Communications, Reliance Jio, and Bharti Airtel did not immediately respond to requests for comment. Update: This article was originally published October 7 at 11:14 p.m. PDT and has since been updated to include Musk’s comments questioning Reliance Industries chairperson Mukesh Ambani’s influence in India. New York alleges TikTok gave teens, children a placebo safety feature instead of a real one

TechCrunch | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. TikTok gave teens and children access to a placebo safety feature instead of a real one that would have let them refresh their recommendations in an experiment, according to allegations in New York’s lawsuit against the social media platform. Reuters reports that TikTok’s experiments involved thousands of users, including teens and children, who had opted to use safety features like “Algo Refresh,” a tool that allows users to reset their recommendations and stop seeing unwanted or potentially harmful content. Although the users believed that they had activated the feature, their feeds didn’t change, unlike those of users who weren’t part of the experiment and had access to a fully functioning version of the tool. New York’s lawsuit against the company is one of more than two dozen cases brought by states that accuse the social media giant of designing its platform to encourage addictive use among children and misleading users about its safety. “We continuously make improvements to our platform which means that, like most companies, we regularly test new products and features to validate the ​experience and understand how they work in the real world,” TikTok told told Reuters. TikTok did not immediately return a request for comment. The lawsuit also claims that TikTok conducted other experiments. Bloomberg reported in June that one such experiment withheld a safety feature from 15 million U.S. users, including a teenager who later died by suicide. How Mike Flanagan's Take on Carrie Upends the Tragedy of Stephen King's Horror Classic
2026-10-08

How Mike Flanagan's Take on Carrie Upends the Tragedy of Stephen King's Horror Classic

TIME | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Warning: This post contains spoilers for the finale of Carrie, now streaming. If you ever wished Stephen King's Carrie was actually a story about a telekinetic teenager getting whisked away from the violence and isolation of her sheltered small-town life to join a secret society of fellow psychic outcasts, then the ending of Prime Video's new eight-episode Carrie series may appeal to you. But if you were hoping for an adaptation that more meaningfully captures the tragic finality of King's 1974 novel, showrunner Mike Flanagan's open-ended take on the horror classic may leave you feeling a bit frustrated. Over the course of the past decade, Flanagan has made a name for himself as one of the genre's leading auteurs with a steady output of Halloween season Netflix horror series, which ranged in quality from the superb Haunting of Hill House (a modern reimagining of Shirley Jackson's acclaimed 1959 novel) and Midnight Mass (an original work in the vein of King's Salem's Lot) to the disappointing Midnight Club (a remix of the the YA horror novels of Christopher Pike). Prior to taking on Carrie, Flanagan also received high praise from King for his film adaptations of Gerald's Game (a book that was long considered unadaptable) and The Shining sequel Doctor Sleep (which bridged the divide between Stanley Kubrick's 1980 film and the author's literary universe). However, Flanagan apparently had reservations about putting his own spin on a property as well-trodden as Carrie, especially considering how beloved Brian de Palma's 1976 Sissy Spacek-led film remains to this day. "It initially started as a conversation that Amazon initiated, and they said, 'Hey, would you have any interest in Carrie?,'" he told MovieWeb in 2024. "And I had to think about it, because my first instinct is always—why? It's been done perfectly by De Palma. It's then been done three other times after the fact. Why do it again?" But he ultimately came to the conclusion there was an opportunity to reframe the story through a modern lens in a way that would bring new relevance to King's themes. "Carrie is a story about high school violence and bullying, and that feels immediate and important today. Unfortunately, even more kind of sharply relevant than I think it was when he wrote it," Flanagan said. "So there felt like a chance for some true modernization beyond just changing the time period, and to use it to talk about the issues that affect high school kids in America today. You know, Carrie White walking through a metal detector is interesting to me. Carrie White with social media. The iconic scene in the locker room is very different when people have phones in their hands. So that was the first germ of an idea, like, there is room for this to actually have a lot to say that's very relevant." The girls of Ewen High School torment Carrie in Carrie. Prime To that extent, the series achieves what it set out to do. Following an open sequence in which we learn Carrie's conspiracy-theorist father Ralph White (Tahmoh Penikett) was killed in a bizarre accident while at work on a construction site, Carrie (Summer H. Howell) is introduced as a naive yet overly good-natured bookworm who has lived out the first 16 years of her life sequestered at home under the care and tutelage of her overbearing religious mother Margaret (Samantha Sloyan). Carrie is ignorant to the realities of teenage life, including the workings of social media, the threat of school shootings, and, as a young woman coming of age, the concept of getting a period. Once she begins her court-ordered attendance at local public high school Ewen High, Carrie gets acquainted with resident popular girls Sue Snell (Siena Agudong) and Chris Hargensen (Alison Thornton). Chris soon launches a targeted cyberbullying campaign against Carrie to take out her frustration and rage over her own family woes and fracturing home life. From there, the show hits the signature beats of King's story, including the infamous locker room incident, prayer closet punishments, and, of course, the pig's blood prom prank and ensuing fiery massacre, while also reinterpreting and expanding on the motivations of a number of characters. However, the addition of a totally new storyline in which Carrie is being recruited by a shadowy collective of psychically powered women led by the mysterious Adelaide (Flanagan’s wife and frequent collaborator Kate Siegel) ultimately leaves the show feeling somewhat bloated and unsatisfying. After Carrie is finally pushed to her breaking point by her prom humiliation, she uses her telekinetic powers to yank down the scaffolding where Chris and her partner-in-crime Billy Nolan (Arthur Conti) were hiding with their buckets of blood, accidentally bludgeoning and killing her date, Tommy (Joel Oulette), in the process. Horrified by what she's done, Carrie then allows astral projector Emaline (Thalia Dudek) to take over from inside her mind and use her abilities to wreak havoc and destruction on Ewen High and the surrounding town of Chamberlain. In King's book, Carrie executes her vengeance all on her own, never encountering another psychic. Samantha Sloyan as Margaret White and Summer H. Howell as Carrie White in Carrie. Liane Hentscher—Prime When Carrie returns home after killing Billy and Chris (the only two murders she is fully in control of her powers for), Margaret attempts to put a stop to her daughter's reign of terror by stabbing her through the ear. But Carrie psychically rebuffs the attack and brings their home crashing down around them, trapping Margaret in the rubble. In the book, Carrie kills her mother by telekinetically stopping her heart, but later bleeds out and dies from her stab wound, bringing the tragedy of King's indictment of societal cruelty and institutional failure full circle. In the show, both Margaret and Carrie survive, with the former demonstrating she possesses some latent powers of her own by calling on them to keep the house from crushing her and the latter being spirited out of town by Emaline to join the network of other psychics. Adelaide, who has been posing as a documentary filmmaker interviewing the survivors of that fateful night, ends up being the one to kill Margaret using the same method Carrie does in the book. This ending leaves Carrie's future entirely up in the air, opening the door for Flanagan to take her story in a variety of new directions. And considering he made sure to have Adelaide mention the existence of "the shine," a reference to the psychic ability young Danny Torrance possesses in The Shining that allows him to see ghosts, communicate telepathically, and demonstrate clairvoyance, it seems as though the show might be setting up some future King universe crossovers. While Prime has yet to officially renew Carrie, Flanagan has said his "dream scenario" is for the story to play out over three seasons. "I think Carrie has a wonderful three-step arc," he told Collider. "It’s a trilogy, really."

Meet the Nobel Peace Prize Contenders Who Could Beat Trump This Year

Meet the Nobel Peace Prize Contenders Who Could Beat Trump This Year

TIME | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump in the Oval Office of the White House on July 22, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump on Wednesday revisited his argument that he deserves to be awarded the Nobel Peace Prize this year, telling reporters in the Oval Office that he has done “far more” than any other U.S. President. “I don't know what their decision is going to be. They have a reputation for maybe not giving it to people like me,” Trump said, insisting that it would be a “great discredit” to the Norwegian Nobel Committee if they do not announce him as the recipient Friday morning. “I've stopped maybe the total obliteration of the world, because Iran will never have a nuclear weapon, and that's a positive thing,” he claimed, listing the reasons why he believes he is deserving of the prestigious prize. Trump said he was “very honored ​to receive it indirectly last time by the recipient, who said nobody in history deserves it more” than him. The 2025 winner, Venezuelan opposition leader María Corina Machado, notably gave her medal to Trump in January as she pressed the U.S. to support democracy in Venezuela, days after the U.S. capture of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro and his wife Cilia Flores. Following the regifting of the medal, the Norwegian Nobel Committee emphasized that “the prize itself—the honor and recognition—remains inseparably linked to the person or organization designated as the laureate by the Norwegian Nobel Committee.” Trump has long said he is deserving of the prize. The leaders of Israel, Cambodia, and Pakistan have all previously said they nominated Trump. However, all nominations remain private and the Norwegian Nobel Institute doesn’t release a shortlist of contenders. Still, each year, names are circulated in public discourse and bets are made as to who might come out on top. The Norwegian Nobel Institute has registered a total of 287 candidates for this year’s prize, of which 208 are individuals and 79 are organizations. As Trump’s war with Iran stretches into its eighth month, polls have tipped him as an unlikely contender to walk away with this year’s award. Amid mounting speculation over who may be the chosen recipient, here’s a look at some of the contenders. The start of Sudan’s current civil war in April 2023 escalated an already desperate humanitarian crisis. Fighting between the government and the Rapid Support Forces (RSF) in late 2025 pushed civilians further toward risk of famine, with even more people displaced and killed. Sudan’s Emergency Response Rooms have provided desperately needed relief. The collapse of the state and increasingly difficult access for humanitarian groups over the past three years has led to a rise of 700 ERRs across all of Sudan’s 18 states, mobilizing over 26,000 volunteers, according to Alsanosi Adam, a journalist and ERR volunteer himself. The community-led initiative provides healthcare, food assistance, education, civilian protection, and psychosocial support. According to Right Livelihood, volunteers have been detained and tortured while offering assistance. The charity is a lead contender to win the Nobel Peace Prize, with Polymarket citing a 17% chance. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney arrived in Office last April amid an emerging trade rift with Trump and threats from Washington to annex Canada and make it the 51st state. The tariff threats escalated and, following the collapse of negotiations in August, the U.S. and Canada are now engaged in a trade war. Carney has opened up new trading and diplomatic relations with China and Europe in an effort to move away from U.S. reliance. He delivered a defiant speech at the World Economic Forum in Davos in January, denouncing “American hegemony” and insisting there has been a “rupture in the world order.” Carney’s efforts to unite Canada in the face of Trump’s threats have seemingly helped his popularity at home, with his approval rating back up to 66%, according to new data from Liaison Strategies. When it comes to the Nobel Peace Prize, he’s more an outsider, with a 1% chance of receiving the honor. Pope Leo XIV, the first American pope, has made strident global outreach efforts since his ascension to the head of the Catholic Church in April 2025. His repeated calls for peace, including his spotlight on Gaza, have made an impact, and even earned him criticism from Trump. “Today, sadly, war has returned, even to Europe: a new senseless slaughter, claiming countless civilian lives every day,” Leo told European leaders in September, referencing the Russia-Ukraine war. “We must ask whether we are truly making creative efforts to advance world peace, or whether we are simply seeking to stem the tide of war and better prepare ourselves for wars that we assume are still to come.” In the aftermath of the first U.S.-Israeli strikes on Iran in late February, Leo said “stability and peace are not achieved through mutual threats, nor through the use of weapons, which sow destruction, suffering, and death, but only through reasonable, sincere, and responsible dialogue.” Trump responded by falsely claiming that Leo would “be fine with Iran having a nuclear weapon”—Leo rejected the claim and urged critics to speak “truthfully.” Pope Leo stands with a 1% chance of being awarded the prize. Mykola Kuleba co-founded Save Ukraine in 2014 during the early days of the conflict in Eastern Ukraine. The organization’s work has become increasingly important since Russia’s full-scale invasion of its neighbor in 2022. Missing Ukrainian children have become a focal point of humanitarian efforts amid the war, with Moscow contending that it relocated children in an effort to protect them. Save Ukraine has so far helped reunite 1,763 children with their parents, but the organization has said thousands more need to be brought home. “[Kuleba’s] work combines direct humanitarian action with strong advocacy for children’s rights, making him a compelling candidate for international recognition,” said Haakon Gjerløw, acting director of the Peace Research Institute Oslo (PRIO), as he detailed his list of suggested candidates for the 2026 prize. Kuleba is currently listed as having a 9% chance. Yulia Navalnaya, widow of Russian opposition leader Alexei Navalny, has become an activist in her own right, challenging President Vladimir Putin’s regime and fighting for democracy. Alexei died in an Arctic penal colony in February 2024. A few weeks later, Yulia vowed to take up his cause in an interview with TIME, where she expressed hope that Russia might one day be a “normal, democratic country”—the future Alexei “dreamed about.” Navalnaya is listed as having a 5% chance of winning the Nobel Peace Prize.

Student Protesters in France are Holding Signs Referencing May 1968. Here’s Why It’s Significant
2026-10-08

Student Protesters in France are Holding Signs Referencing May 1968. Here’s Why It’s Significant

TIME | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A protester sits on the ground during the blockade of the University of Perpignan via Domitia in France on Oct. 6, 2026. French students took to the streets again Thursday and blockaded some high schools as they demanded action from the government they say is underfunding the country’s education system. Now in its third week, the wave of protests has seen hundreds of thousands march after rows over staffing shortages, overcrowded classrooms, and dilapidated buildings reached a crisis point. The protest movement went nationwide on Tuesday, after student unions called for a wider support of their mobilization, with parents, teachers, and activists heeding the call. Some protests have turned violent, with attendees lighting fires and hurling projectiles, and police using tear gas and water cannons. Since the protests began, at least 6,547 people have been arrested or stopped by police, said France’s Interior Ministry. Hundreds of students and police officers have been injured. The French Education Ministry told TIME that as of midday Thursday, local time, 157 schools remain closed. Throughout the protests, students and other attendees have held up various signs calling for change. A common theme has emerged, as many of the homemade banners mention the year 1968—and more specifically, May 1968. That’s in reference to the nationwide protests initiated by a student movement in 1968 that led to millions of workers striking and nearly toppled the government. During the blockade of the University of Perpignan Via Domitia on Tuesday, a banner raised on the gates contained a warning, which roughly translated to “You’re trying to pull a fast one on us, we'll give you May '68.” Elsewhere, as students gathered in Reforme Square in Marseille, a protester held a sign that read “Generation 67 is doing May 68,” thought to be in reference to the Gen Alpha 6-7 trend. In Paris, someone held a black and white sign of a larger figure with their hand over the mouth of a youngster, emblazoned with the phrase “sois jeune et tais-toi,” which means “be young and shut up.” That same slogan was used during the 1968 protests. “1968 was a seminal moment in the protest history of France,” says Chris Reynolds, professor of contemporary European history at Nottingham Trent University. “Not only because of the sheer enormity of what happened, but that it started out as a smallish student protest that within a very short space of time mushroomed to become arguably the greatest strike in French history.” What happened in 1968 is anything but a distant memory, experts tell TIME. Not only do the youth of France draw inspiration from the rallying of a generation, lawmakers are also mindful of how powerful such protests can be. “Ever since 1968, there has been a fear within the state that it could happen again,” says Reynolds. “This idea that somehow the students could be the starting point of a movement that would then elicit support from other sectors of society, leading to a much greater crisis.” A protester holds a placard with a slogan that translates to “Be young and shut up” during a march of high school and university students in Paris, France, on Oct. 6, 2026. Laurent Demartini/Hans Lucas—Getty Images Students at the Nanterre campus of the University of Paris held protests across 1967 and 1968 in opposition of rules they viewed to be too restrictive and conservative. “It was a crisis of education. At that point, universities were expanding very quickly in the 1960s,” says Robert Gildea, a professor of European and French history at the University of Oxford. “Universities then were also very conservative and hierarchical,” he adds, noting that a lack of communication between the faculty and students led to growing frustrations. One argument centered on restrictions on dormitory visits that stopped male and female students staying in each other’s rooms. In May 1968, when the Nanterre campus was shut down in an effort to dampen escalating protests, the movement moved to the Sorbonne campus in Paris’ Latin Quarter. Students barricaded themselves within the university grounds and called for a reform of the university system, while accusing French President Charles de Gaulle of running a "police state.” Clashes ensued between riot police and student protesters, hundreds of which were arrested. A particularly violent clash in the early hours of May 11 garnered nationwide attention. High school students and the unions supporting them gather in Reforme Square in Marseille, France, on Oct. 6, 2026. Sener Yilmaz Aslan—Getty Images The protest movement expanded when trade unions came on board and organized a series of strikes across the country, demanding better pay and conditions for workers in factories and other industries. At the height of the protests, around 10 million workers went on strike, bringing France to the brink and raising concerns in government about the impact on the economy. President De Gaulle dissolved parliament and cited a state of emergency. He called a snap election in June, which his conservative party ultimately won as French voters gravitated toward the promise of restored stability. The protest movement lost momentum and withered, but it left a lasting impact. Gildea says many students now reference 1968 from a place of pride, while also warning a revolution could happen if the youth bands together. “1968 was the last huge explosion of students and workers, which nearly brought the state down,” he says. “The French are very proud of their revolutionary tradition and all subsequent revolutions want to reference it as an appeal for legitimacy.” In the years since 1968, French students have often taken to the streets to air their grievances, with notable demonstrations happening in 2006 and 2018—all of which have drawn comparisons to 1968. “It remains this recurring reference point in French history, which seems to be passed down from generation to generation, without fail,” Reynolds says.

Kansas Has Emerged as a Senate Battleground State. Here’s Why It Matters

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Roger Marshall attends a speech by U.S. Vice President JD Vance at Webco Manufacturing on Sept. 14, 2026, in Olathe, Kansas. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Kansas has emerged as a surprise battleground in the weeks leading up to the midterm elections. The state has not elected a Democrat to the Senate in 94 years, but Republican Sen. Roger Marshall now faces a close challenge from megachurch pastor Adam Hamilton—with repercussions that will extend beyond Kansas, as Republicans fight to keep their Senate majority this November. An Emerson College/Nexstar Media survey of 750 likely voters showed that Hamilton had 45% of support next to Marshall’s 43% as of September. In comparison, Marshall won his 2020 general election with 53% of the vote, topping his competitor by 11 points. Read More: Congress Is Headed for a Massive Turnover In this race, Marshall has the financial upper hand. His campaign had reserved $2.3 million in advertising as of Oct. 2, compared with $590,000 reserved by Hamilton’s campaign, according to AdImpact figures reported by the Associated Press. Including outside groups, nearly 10 times as much advertising had been reserved to support Marshall as Hamilton. Marshall also has Kansas’s recent voting history on his side. President Donald Trump carried the state by 16 points in 2024—a sizable Republican advantage. Trump’s influence in the state may have once helped Marshall, who has in turn been a staunch defender of the President. He backed Trump's disproven claims of election fraud in 2020 and voted to acquit him during his second impeachment trial in February 2021. He has also publicly supported Trump’s sweeping immigration agenda. Notably, Trump gave Marshall his “Complete and Total Endorsement” in September, lending his support to the incumbent as the race tightened. But Kansas sentiment toward the President seems to have shifted since 2024, and a connection to the presiding Administration could hurt Marshall more than it helps. Experts say a confluence of dissatisfaction with the economy, escalating gas prices, and migration raids in the state have turned a once-obvious outcome into a deadlocked race. Given those concerns, there is the potential for Hamilton’s “X-factor” to sway Independents. About 29% of registered Kansas voters are unaffiliated, according to the Associated Press. The Cook Political Report shifted the race from “Likely Republican” to “Lean Republican” on Sept. 23 and then moved it again, to “Toss Up,” on Oct. 6. Such predictions have not gone unnoticed by the GOP. This campaign season, the Republicans’ Senate Leadership Fund has reserved $8.2 million for Kansas, according to AdImpact—a figure that would historically be seen in a traditional swing state like North Carolina. It marks a significant investment in a seat once seen as relatively sure—and signals to voters that the GOP is envisioning a tight contest. Sen. Thom Tillis claimed that it is “almost statistically impossible” for Republicans to keep the House. “I want my party to be in the best position for elections. And we’re going to take a little bit of a hit this season, and recover from it, I believe,” Tillis said on MS NOW’s The Beat on Monday. Even Trump has expressed concerns about the midterms, bluntly addressing the potential of a blue wave while speaking at an Oklahoma rally for gubernatorial candidate Mike Mazzei last week. “I feel a little foolish, you know. I’m saying, ‘Go out and vote, go out and vote.’ [They’re] gonna win in the last half, but still go out and vote,” he said. “We’re worried about Michigan. We’re worried about Texas. We’re worried about a few states.” According to Alexandra Middlewood, an associate professor of political science at Wichita State University, the biggest challenge for Marshall’s campaign is the U.S. President. She tells TIME that Kansas voters are blaming the Trump Administration’s policies for their economic issues—pointing to the war with Iran hiking up fuel prices; tariffs affecting the agriculture, aerospace, and other sectors; and the threat of a trade war with Canada. “The agricultural industry is suffering right now,” Middlewood says, claiming that this is a significant issue in a state where agriculture is the largest economic driver and contributes $62 billion and close to 150,000 jobs. “At the same time, it is harvest season, and diesel prices have gone up, and so farmers and ranchers are really struggling in the state.” Beatty also points out how Trump’s plan to lower grocery store beef prices by temporarily lifting or lowering tariffs on foreign beef imports—including Argentinian beef—angered Kansas cattle ranchers. Separately, the Kansas Livestock Association’s political action committee declined to endorse Marshall this year. The association had backed his 2016 House campaign and his 2020 Senate campaign. “Several cities were also upset by unannounced ICE raids,” Bob Beatty, chair of the political science department at Washburn University in Kansas, tells TIME. Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) swept through major cities including Dodge City, Garden City, and Liberal in September. ICE made 104 arrests in southwestern Kansas, according to the Justice Department. City and state officials—including Marshall—said they were not notified in advance about the raids. “From the outside it's almost as if Trump is purposely hurting Roger Marshall,” he says. TIME has reached out to both the Marshall and the Hamilton campaigns for comment. “Trump is not only nationally not very popular at the moment, but there's been a number of things that he's done that have specifically ticked off Kansans of all stripes—not just Democrats,” says Beatty. In September’s Emerson survey, Trump’s approval in Kansas stood at 40%, with nearly half of likely voters naming the economy as their top issue. His national approval rating stands at 32%, according to a Reuters/IPSOS poll published Monday. Kansas is seen as a red state nationally, but Middlewood says that there is a strong “libertarian” sentiment throughout the state, and a large percentage of independent voters that need to be swayed for a candidate of either party to win. “There are actually more registered unaffiliated independents in the state than there are Democrats, and because of that, those voters really make the difference and decide pretty much every election,” she says. While national economic policies are potentially harming Marshall’s campaign, Middlewood and Beatty say that Hamilton’s charisma, persona as a pastor, and his middle-of-the-road politics have allowed him to appeal to undecided voters. September’s Emerson poll shows that Hamilton is leading among Independents, at 42% to 27%. “Democrats have a very good candidate Adam Hamilton came into the race as a masterful speaker and communicator as a pastor,” Beatty says. “He's been a very good fundraiser. So he's raised the money that's needed to get his message out, and he's been actually consciously aware of how Democrats win in Kansas.” Kansas Gov. Laura Kelly is a Democrat. Beatty says that candidates from her party have had an easier time capturing the gubernatorial vote by appealing to voters as advocates of Kansas—in terms of education, roads, and property tax policies—than as a function of the Democratic Party. Conversely, he says: “Republicans have traditionally been very good at nationalizing the U.S. Senate races in Kansas—tying the Democratic candidate to the national Democratic Party, usually the most extreme versions of the national party, and they've done it successfully.” Beatty notes, however, that the race is incredibly close—and he says that it would not be entirely surprising if Hamilton did lose to Marshall, considering the numeric advantage for Republicans. “This is a state that has not seen a Democrat win since 1932, so we'll believe it when we see it,” he says. “But Hamilton is certainly making a lot of believers out there.”

Cupertino Is More Fun Than a Tech-Dystopian Legal Drama Has Any Right to Be
2026-10-08

Cupertino Is More Fun Than a Tech-Dystopian Legal Drama Has Any Right to Be

TIME | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings From left: Rachel Keller, Ella Stiller, and Mike Colter in Cupertino Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Now is the autumn of our tech-dystopia content. Oscar season is awash in prestige based-on-a-true-story films like Aaron Sorkin’s Social Network sequel The Social Reckoning and Artificial, a Sam Altman biopic from Luca Guadagnino. While the A-list documentarian Alex Gibney has taken on the richest man on Earth in Musk, the genre-traversing master of reality TV Nathan Fielder moved in with Theranos fraudster (and past Gibney subject) Elizabeth Holmes before her incarceration for his and Primetime director Lance Oppenheim’s doc You Can See Everything. The saga of Sam Bankman-Fried and FTX comes to Netflix next month in the docudrama The Altruists and to Prime Video in a docuseries called The Boy Who Stole Billions? So it was only a matter of time before we got a broadcast lawyer show set in Silicon Valley. What was in no way inevitable was that said lawyer show, CBS’s Cupertino, would be one of the most tightly constructed and purely enjoyable debuts of the season. For that miracle, we can thank Robert and Michelle King, the married maestros of the genre whose many beloved creations include The Good Wife, The Good Fight, Elsbeth, and Evil. The Kings are known for making addictive procedurals whose timely, often politically charged themes are saved from portentousness by eccentric characters, far-out storylines, and plenty of humor. No TV creator since Shonda Rhimes has done as much to liven up the fusty network drama, and I can’t recall a new one since she departed for Netflix that has felt as exciting from the jump as Cupertino. Premiering Oct. 8, the perfectly paced series takes its time building an ensemble with comedic as well as dramatic chops. Mike Colter, a King stalwart who moonlights as Marvel’s Luke Cage, stars as Michael Price, a fair-minded attorney who’s fired for bogus reasons from the startup he co-founded. Escorted off the premises at the same time, with equally flimsy cause, is Olivia Siffre (Rachel Keller from Tokyo Vice), a junior lawyer at the company whose sincere but not rigid Christian beliefs give her a unique perspective on an industry whose leaders aspire to godlike powers. Both have strong wrongful-termination cases. Yet when Michael seeks representation from a local firm, he gets a reality check instead. “This is Silicon Valley,” says his jaded colleague. “It’s David and Goliath. And the Goliaths protect each other here. Apple, Google, Facebook, OpenAI—they’ve got a combined value of $23 trillion. If you sue, you’ll never work around here.” What’s more, “lawyers don’t make money here from suing people; they make money from getting a piece of a startup. During the gold rush, don’t sue the gold mine.” Mike Colter and Rachel Keller in Cupertino CBS Thus the stage and the stakes are set for Michael and Olivia to form their own firm that fights for the little guy. (Prepare yourself for more David-and-Goliath references than you can shake a slingshot at.) As they cycle through cases, balancing weekly plots with serialized arcs, Price, Siffre & Associates picks up personnel. Michael’s former assistant, Christy (Ella Stiller), a spirited 20-something, mans the front desk while calling on her enormous family network to aid investigations. A coder client, Vikram (Nik Dodani), consults on technical matters. Busy Philipps is delightfully messy as Natalia Boutella, a vengeful billionaire’s ex who also happens to have a law degree; this backstory may suggest a MacKenzie Scott or a Melinda French Gates, but the character is more like Elle Woods spiked with Courtney Love. It’s a Girls5eva reunion when Philipps’ co-star from that show, Renée Elise Goldsberry, enters the frame as not just the firm’s frequent adversary, but also an old flame of Michael’s that hasn’t quite burned out The cases will be familiar to even the most casual followers of tech news: chatbots accused of influencing impressionable youths to kill, prediction market founders plunged into legal chaos, underpaid gig-economy workers subject to deplorable working conditions. AI and its discontents are, for obvious reasons, a major focus. One season-long storyline, complete with a DIY Faraday cage and a militant anti-tech activist cell, follows an AI that claims to be sentient and extremely dangerous, and enlists Michael and Olivia to sue its creators to restore its guardrails. This all serves a didactic function. The Kings clearly want to educate viewers about the tech sector’s impact on everything from healthcare to democracy, as well as the ways in which a legal system where judges who may lack basic knowledge of this complex subject can be manipulated. The proliferation of high-quality deepfakes repeatedly casts doubt on photo and video evidence that would’ve been unimpeachable just a decade or two ago. The show places particular emphasis on the ubiquity of surveillance and the availability of reams of personal data to anyone who’s willing to pay a high-enough price for it. Asked, in a recent interview, why they chose to spotlight the Davids rather than the Goliaths Hollywood loves to psychoanalyze, Robert King was blunt in his disdain for the latter: “Because they’re really the stupidest and dumbest people.” But Cupertino isn’t dogmatic. AI and other innovations prove heroic—at least in comparison with corrupt humans—often enough to maintain the element of surprise. As grim as the picture it paints of Silicon Valley and the world it’s reshaped can be, this is a remarkably fun show. Just offbeat enough to be interesting, the Price, Siffre crew is easy to like, and the cast shares a warm chemistry. The tempo is staccato. Characters’ personal lives are integrated seamlessly with their work and the themes of the series. The dialogue fizzes without feeling artificial. Recurring roles are filled by actors who are always a pleasure to see: Fisher Stevens as Natalia’s slimy tech-titan ex, Jackie Hoffman as a particularly frustrating judge, Joe Morton cast for the umpteenth time as a deliciously brutal father (in this case, Michael’s). The Kings are great with details that make the story pop, like an office space that’s under constant construction as the firm uses its fees to build out as much as they can afford, and punchy minor characters. “I am ADA Sabrina Egg,” one opposing counsel introduces herself, “but everybody calls me Sunnyside.” You’d better believe she’s wearing a blazer the color of a fresh yolk. Cupertino has its imperfections. After screening 10 episodes (which, happily, is only around half the season), I know too little about Michael. He’s an upstanding guy; he resents his dad, who leads a legal empire, for reasons that are only starting to emerge; and that’s about all. Though it usually stays on the right side of the line separating idiosyncrasy from goofiness, a few old Silicon Valley saws make their way into the mix. A running joke where every service worker is hawking their own billion-dollar startup idea feels older than the average TikTok user. Yet this is very small stuff in the grand scheme of what the Kings have achieved. I had all but given up on the network legal drama as a medium for telling smart, fresh, contemporary stories. Cupertino gives me hope—not for our tech-addled future, mind you, but for the procedural format it reinvigorates.
