'I Think I'm the Best Player in the NBA.' The Excellence of Shai Gilgeous-Alexander
2026-10-08

'I Think I'm the Best Player in the NBA.' The Excellence of Shai Gilgeous-Alexander

TIME | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Shai Gilgeous-Alexander had not seen the Onion headline. We are talking in the back of a black SUV, parked in front of his offseason home in the Toronto suburbs, when I mention the bit of satire that went viral during the Western Conference Finals in May. I hand him my phone and he laughs. “Shai Gilgeous-Alexander Trying to Draw Foul While Shooting Free Throw,” reads the headline, which is accompanied by an image of him flailing on the foul line. “Trying to draw a foul at the free-throw line,” he says, “that would be good.” Gilgeous-Alexander, or SGA, is known for many things—winning NBA MVP two years in a row; becoming one of the only players in league history to sweep the regular-season MVP, scoring title, and Finals MVP in the same year (Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, Michael Jordan, and Shaquille O’Neal are the others); being selected four times for both the All-Star team and the All-NBA First Team; and being named the 2026 Clutch Player of the Year. But the Oklahoma City Thunder superstar who has achieved initial status—think MJ, Dr. J, or KD—has also faced backlash. Fellow players, pundits, and the NBA’s chaotic online tinderbox have accused him of “flopping”—or acting as if he were hit much harder than he really was, often falling down in the process, to goad the ref into calling a foul. The goal: get to the free-throw line for two easy points. San Antonio’s 7-ft. 4-in. alien talent Victor Wembanyama helped unleash this anger in December when he said, after a Spurs win over the Thunder, that he was glad to suit up for a team that practiced “pure and ethical basketball.” The implication was clear: OKC is a bunch of foul merchants, with Gilgeous-Alexander, who led the NBA in free-throws-made per game for a third straight season, the top salesman. The criticism only escalated when the Thunder faced the Spurs for a spot in the Finals. I want to gauge SGA’s feelings about all the noise. Up to this point, he has seemed almost universally admired, if not beloved—perhaps by dint of his businesslike demeanor, low-wattage Canadian upbringing, and small-market employer. Understandably, given his 30-plus-points-per-game average, 88% efficiency from the line over the past three regular seasons and playoffs combined, plethora of honors, and the $285 million contract extension he signed in July 2025, SGA is unapologetic about his approach to scoring. “Playing this game has done amazing things for my and my family’s life,” he says. “And if a narrative on social media is the worst that comes from this game for me, well, I’d take that a million times out of a million times.” Besides, he has other priorities at the moment. Last season, Oklahoma City was on the brink of establishing a dynasty. They were the reigning NBA champions, having won a title in 2025, and had again finished with the league’s best record. Then the Spurs sent them home in that contentious seven-game series. “Awful feeling, sick to your stomach,” SGA recalls. “I tried to let it go for a little bit. Don’t obsess over it. Not yet.” The Thunder are now the co-favorites with the Spurs—against whom they face off in the season opener on Oct. 20—to win the 2027 title, but other teams are hogging the spotlight. The New York Knicks are enjoying much of the shine after bringing the championship trophy home after a 53-year drought, and could become the first franchise in nearly a decade to repeat. Meanwhile the Philadelphia 76ers, on paper, have assembled a superteam, as LeBron James and Jaylen Brown join forces with 2023 league MVP Joel Embiid, two-time All-Star Tyrese Maxey, and VJ Edgecombe, who finished third in 2026 Rookie of the Year voting. Gilgeous-Alexander, 28, is used to being overlooked: he’s morphed from an underhyped draft pick, and the consolation prize in a blockbuster trade, to arguably the best basketball player on earth. Through spectacularly consistent work, he has mastered basketball physics, starting and stopping and slithering his way into the lane enough to, in March, break Wilt Chamberlain’s record for most consecutive games scoring at least 20 points (126). “One person saw this happening,” says Thunder coach Mark Daigneault, who was an assistant coach when SGA joined the team in 2019. “And it was him. That’s it. If anybody else says, ‘This is what I saw,’ then it’s revisionist history.” SGA wants to strike that dutiful descriptor—arguably—from the record. “I think I’m the best player in the NBA,” he says. “I think the production that I have night in and night out is best-player-in-the-NBA production. I think the efficiency that I do it at is best-player-in-the-NBA efficiency. I think everybody picks up a basketball or a soccer ball or a football or a tennis ball to win the game, the match, whatever it is. And I’ve done my fair share of winning in these past couple years.” SGA won his first title on June 22, 2025, when the Thunder beat the Indiana Pacers Jesse D. Garrabrant—NBAE/Getty Images SGA credits his hometown of Hamilton, Ontario—“an underdog city”—for his work ethic, which is helpful considering the two-time NBA MVP didn’t make the JV team in ninth grade. Born to Vaughn Alexander, who won a high school city championship in Toronto, and Charmaine Gilgeous, who represented Antigua and Barbuda in the 400 m at the 1992 Barcelona Olympics, he and his younger brother Thomasi sampled all kinds of sports, including the national pastime of hockey—though never on ice. “I wanted to, but I just couldn’t afford it,” he says. But by high school, SGA was all in on basketball. So it was a “wake-up call” when he was cut from the squad. “I thought I was this player,” he says. “I learned quickly that I wasn’t.” (In an oft-told tale, Michael Jordan’s high school coach cut him from the varsity team as a sophomore, fueling his ferocious competitiveness: the man was booed when introduced at a Jordan salute in Chicago in the mid-1990s, and Jordan referenced the slight in his Hall of Fame speech. “You made a mistake, dude,” Jordan said. SGA, on the other hand, doesn’t even recall this coach’s name. “I’ll let him off the hook,” he says.) He sprouted from 5 ft. 7 in., with big clown feet, in ninth grade to 6 ft. 2 in. at the end of 10th. But, he says, “I was wonky. I was growing with knee pains, moving slow, hard to control my body.” So SGA and his club-basketball coach Dwayne Washington, a high school math and science teacher, spent hours in the gym developing tricks to compensate for his lack of coordination: superior ballhandling, changing speeds to deceive defenders, perfecting the art of deceleration—stopping fast on a dribble, so a defender’s momentum carries him past you. He wrote down practice instructions in a marble notebook. “He is able to sustain concentration for longer periods of time than anybody I’ve seen,” says Washington. “Everything is predicated on his inner clock to slow down, focus on tasks, and problem solve.” By the time SGA matured into his 6-ft. 6-in. frame, and became more athletic and efficient in his movements, those tricks had evolved into lethal weapons. In order to face better competition, he and his first cousin, Atlanta Hawks guard Nickeil Alexander-Walker, who grew up in Toronto, played their last two years of high school basketball together in the U.S. They led Hamilton Heights Christian Academy, in Chattanooga, Tenn., to a Christian schools national championship. “Being able to hold your own in America is the first step to like, ‘OK, I’m on the right track,’” says SGA. The next stop was the University of Kentucky, where he planned to do one year before entering the NBA Draft. He feels fortunate that NIL—the legal right of college athletes to cash in on their name, image, and likeness—wasn’t on the books then. Having so many options and so much money in college can sap a player’s urgency to collect a pro payday. “For me, it was so simple,” he says. “Now it’s tricky. You’re right out of high school. You don’t know anything. It’s hard to make smart business decisions. Kids have handlers, but you see it all the time: guys go down the wrong path because they’re advised by the wrong people. There’s room for error.” At 19, after one year in college, SGA was picked 11th overall in the 2018 NBA Draft Mike Lawrie—Getty Images In the 2018 draft, the Charlotte Hornets selected SGA with the 11th overall pick, then traded him to the Los Angeles Clippers minutes later. He had a promising rookie season, scoring 10.8 points per game, and finished sixth in Rookie of the Year voting. But Gilgeous-Alexander, along with the Clippers’ third leading scorer, Danilo Gallinari, and a boatload of future first-round draft choices, was shipped to the Thunder for Paul George, who had finished third in the MVP voting that season. “You’re never as good as you think you are, or as safe or as comfortable as you think you are,” he says. “I just use it as a chip on my shoulder.” Playing with Hall of Famer Chris Paul in his first OKC season further enhanced his development. “I used to eat terrible,” says Gilgeous-Alexander, who strongly believes that Canadian McDonald’s is better than American McDonald’s, thank you very much. “The seriousness that he approached the things outside of the game, his diet, the weight room, recovery, everything that he did ticked towards his performance. That was pretty eye-opening to me.” In March 2020, the pandemic shut down sports and SGA returned to Ontario. When he came back to OKC for pre-bubble workouts in June, he was visibly stronger. “It was COVID, everybody’s on Zoom calls, staying in their pajamas all day,” says Daigneault. “You’re like, ‘OK, maybe he’s in his pajamas, but if he’s in his pajamas, he’s lifting.’ He was not resting.” On a vet-laden playoff team that earned a five seed in the loaded Western Conference, it was SGA, the sophomore, who topped the Thunder in scoring. In Gilgeous-Alexander, the Thunder had a foundational piece. But the team would take its lumps. “We had to take a longer view to get to a more sustainable level of winning,” says OKC general manager Sam Presti. The franchise lost 50 games in 2020–21 and 58 in 2021–22, before creeping back near .500, 40-42, in 2022–23. But for Presti, those years were productive: He cleared salary-cap space and used the first-round picks from the Clippers deal—plus the Thunder’s own selections—to stockpile young standouts like Jalen Williams and Chet Holmgren, both drafted in 2022, and use others as trade assets. This fallow period also put the Thunder in position to better compensate their emerging stars and to add veteran role players like Isaiah Hartenstein, who joined the team in 2024. The losing seasons never got SGA down. “I get to go out there and essentially shoot any ball that I want,” he says. “So many players don’t get this opportunity. I was so wrapped up in just playing the game that I love with that amount of responsibility that it was honestly just so fun for me that I didn’t even care.” He was also enjoying himself off the court, establishing a reputation as one of the world’s best-dressed athletes. He showcased his fashion sense before and after Thunder games, at the Met Gala, and even on a Paris Fashion Week runway. “It’s fun to be able to play with color palettes,” says Gilgeous-Alexander, who has arrived at arenas in all-leather outfits, oversize fur coats, and various presentations of camo. “I’ve always been into arts and crafts, and it’s a way to do it through clothes.” He beat out Timothée Chalamet and A$AP Rocky for GQ’s Most Stylish Man of 2022, well before the Thunder became a national TV staple. “My parents always made sure that we looked presentable,” he says. “That was a nonnegotiable. Little things, whether it’s making sure your pants are seamed the right way, or the collar was flapped the right way.” Shai, photographed in Toronto on Sept. 4, spends his offseasons in the area Photography by Sharif Hamza for TIME SGA has inspired his teammates to look good too. “He’s made me spend more money, for sure,” quips Williams. The young, chic Thunder developed a unique chemistry. Gilgeous-Alexander and Holmgren sang an off-key parody of Christina Aguilera’s “What a Girl Wants” in an AT&T commercial that ran ad nauseam during March Madness and the NBA playoffs in 2024; the next year Williams joined them in a new spot, the joke being they all wore the same outfit to an event. Players also developed a tradition of crashing postgame TV interviews with Thunder sideline reporter Nick Gallo, covering him in towels and executing other hijinks as he talks to the star of the game. SGA sets the tone. “I was shocked at how normal he is,” says Williams. “It makes it really easy for people on the team to feel like they can be themselves.” As the accolades have piled up, he’s remained committed to meshing. “If you’re a superstar in the NBA, there’s a natural gravity that pulls you outside the team,” says Daigneault. “He wants to be one of the guys as badly as he wants to be an all-time player. He’s constantly plugging himself back into the team despite the gravity that’s pulling him out.” After each of his MVP announcements, SGA bought gifts for his teammates: Rolexes in 2025; Audemars Piguet watches, Burberry trench coats, and golf clubs in May. “These guys don’t get to have as much fun,” he says. “I don’t ever have to think about my minutes. I don’t have to think about where my shots are coming from. Essentially, we go out there, we play my game. These guys rebound, dive on loose balls, do all the dirty work. They essentially play off of me. I just think that they deserve a little bit of credit for their sacrifice.” SGA was awarded his second straight NBA MVP trophy before a playoff game on May 18 Alex Slitz—Getty Images After the Thunder won 68 regular-season games in 2024–25 and beat their opponents by an average of 12.9 points per game, a new NBA record, they began this past season 24-1, tying the 2015–16 Golden State Warriors for the hottest start ever. But injuries plagued them in the Western Conference Finals, as Williams and Ajay Mitchell, a second-year player who provided punch off the bench, missed key games. The 7-ft. 1-in. Holmgren had a particularly rough series: Wembanyama’s production (27.3 points per game) trumped Holmgren’s (10.7 points), and the Frenchman dunked on Holmgren minutes into Game 7. Holmgren finished that final game with 4 points, 4 rebounds, and no assists. Among fans and pundits, he took a beating. But on the September day we spend together in Ontario, Gilgeous-Alexander says he was the one at fault. “The MVP of the league waited until Game 7 to have a good game,” he says. (While SGA’s output and efficiency did not meet his usual standard, he did shoot 50% from the field and score 30 points in OKC’s Game 2 victory over San Antonio.) “I don’t think it’s on Chet,” he says. “I carry a lot of the weight. The only way you can truly fail is if you don’t learn from the moment. I know the type of guy he is. And I know what type of worker he is. He’ll definitely do it.” About two weeks later, I tell Holmgren that SGA blamed himself for the defeat. “I might disagree with him,” he replies. “Losing in a sh-tty fashion on the last game of the season, and f-ck-ing having that be your last game for the next three months or whatever, sucks. But he definitely checked on me. We chopped it up, trying to talk some hoops, figuring out how to use this time of the summer productively. I don’t think I’m somebody who necessarily needs to be checked in on. But it speaks to his character and his willingness and awareness to do that.” Off Queen Elizabeth Way, not far from a Ford assembly plant that drives what’s left of Ontario’s manufacturing economy, sits the gym where Gilgeous-Alexander holes up all summer. It’s an unremarkable space—from time to time, SGA says, a shot bounces off the facility’s low ceiling. A half-dozen young men join him, a mix of former college players who offer some defensive resistance and smaller guys he’s known since grade school. They rebound and do some passing. “That’s his circle,” says Nate Mitchell, an assistant coach for the Canadian national team who works with SGA in the summers. “It doesn’t get any bigger than what you see.” While most Ontarians are just waking up, SGA sweats through his workout, shooting dozens of three-pointers on the move. Over the past couple of seasons, he has upped his three-point attempts and efficiency, making him even more difficult to defend, though the long ball abandoned him in the Spurs series. “It’s OK, it’s OK,” he says while bobbling a pass, burying the shot anyway. He showed up at 6 a.m. this morning, but these sessions can start as early as 4:30. The week prior, Gilgeous-Alexander flew home from Nike’s campus outside Portland, Ore., slept two hours, and was on the court before dawn. (He transferred from Converse, a Nike subsidiary, to the big brand in June; Nike plans to release a second edition of his signature shoe—the Shai 002—early next year. Asked what customers can expect, SGA, who counts drawing and tennis as hobbies, responds: “Sick design. Because I drew it. That’s all I can give you.”) After a series of drills, he is bent over, hands on knees. “A little fatigue never hurt nobody,” says Mitchell. Gilgeous-Alexander steps to his safe space, the foul line, where he’s almost automatic. He makes nine in a row. For the record, he does not practice falling. Yahoo! Sports basketball analyst Tom Haberstroh did yeoman’s work last spring: he tracked more than 1,300 shots taken by top scorers SGA, Wembanyama, Jalen Brunson, James Harden, and Donovan Mitchell in the playoffs and found that, indeed, SGA hits the deck at the highest rate—on both attempts that are whistled as fouls and those that aren’t. But SGA swears he’s not acting. After all, he drives to the bucket more than most, so he’s more likely to draw contact. Plus, he often lands on his tush for safety. “Sometimes I’m leaning so much and fading away so much that it’s easier for me to fall than to try to stick a landing or risk landing on someone,” he says. “It’s just natural. Your body’s in a way in the air that if you try to stick the landing, it’s unnatural. If I’m this way and my knee will have to go the other way, you’re compromising your body.” With Echo, the Doberman he has called the “swaggiest pup ever” Photography by Sharif Hamza for TIME Even with SGA healthy, OKC is no lock to top the Western Conference. Younger players like Ajay Mitchell and Cason Wallace must keep improving. And Presti traded some battle-tested cogs this summer in order to save salary and load up on more draft picks. One of them, Lu Dort, is SGA’s running mate on the Canadian national team. He’s been going up against Gilgeous-Alexander in practice for years. “I keep telling people I’m the only one that can stop him,” says Dort, who will now play with SGA’s cousin on the Hawks. SGA sees things differently. “He’s going to stand in front of me and try to stop me from going places on the basketball court, and I’m going to go places on the basketball court,” he says. “That’s just how basketball works.” After SGA finishes his workout, he makes small talk with his team about movies—SGA has never watched a mob flick—and whether they prefer Damian Lillard or Kyrie Irving in their primes (he pleads the fifth). He’ll go back home for a lift and to hang with Ares, his 2-year-old son with his high school sweetheart and now wife Hailey Summers. As he looks ahead to the season, SGA makes clear that he’s not out for revenge. “It’s hard to be in that state of mind that long,” he says later in the day. “Even as a kid, I was never good when I played mad. Never, never, never, never. I just don’t think you make the best decisions.” Instead, he’ll lean on his curious mind. He’s making tactical adjustments he won’t share. (Trade secrets.) He’ll troubleshoot at his own pace. “I wasn’t happy to lose,” SGA says. “But I was like, ‘OK, good, I deserved it.’ It’s my job to figure out why I didn’t deserve to win. I attack losing.” Like many elite athletes, SGA dabbles in other pursuits. He is a part-owner of an arena in Hamilton, and according to his management team, he has also joined the ownership groups of the Spanish soccer club Atlético Madrid and the coffee brand La La Land. In September, he started a charitable foundation, and he plans on launching a media and entertainment company in early 2027. (Gilgeous-Alexander is less inclined to involve himself in politics. During the peak of the U.S.-Canada tariff war in early September, he wasn’t much interested in opining on North American trade policy, though he did offer a brief comment on President Trump’s attempt to make Lake America a thing. “It’s obviously Lake Ontario to me,” he says. “I don’t have nothing more to say beyond that.”) But despite his many engagements, SGA assures fans that the Thunder come first. “I mean, you can’t put your time into everything in life,” he says. “I should get started sooner than later, to figure out what I’ll do after [basketball]. But while I’m in it, it gets all of me.” Beyond his on-court prowess, SGA has established himself as one of the world's most stylish athletes Photography by Sharif Hamza for TIME Styling: Matthew Henson; Clothing: Gucci; jewelry: talent’s own; watch: Audemars Piguet; Set Design: Suzanne Campos; Grooming: Lateisha Grant; Location: Astrolab Studio.

I've personally seen this is in a bunch of situations. Here's a personal example, much smaller than anything like these problems. I have a recent preprint with a student here https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36068 [arxiv.org] (about 90% of this was done by her. She's very good.) But part of this came from when I ran a version of Proposition 13 in that paper through Claude just to clean up the draft of that bit before I sent it to her. Claude informed me (essentially unprompted) that the argument had a whole in it (in addition to pointing out grammar errors, unbalanced parentheses and some other embarrassing minor mistakes). I then fixed the hole, and gave it back to Claude. Claude thought for a few minutes, and then informed me that I had *not* fixed the hole, and the reason was that my version of the proposition was missing an entire infinite family which it constructed. The literature on this problem is small, and I'm very familiar with it. The family it produced is straightforward (see Prop 8), but definitely was not in the existing literature. So yes, these systems really can do novel math, and can even do so in an essentially minimally prompted fashion, in this case, explaining to the meat mathematician why his proof is wrong. My student and I then generalized Claude's family to Theorem 9 in that paper, but the AI definitely had an impact. At some level this was actually not a good thing. I'm trying to encourage students to *not* rely on the AI for research so they develop basic research skills. So if the AI had not volunteered the family I wouldn't have had to tell her that the AI had discovered it, but honestly required noting it. I've only had time to start looking at two of them. First, is the Hadwiger-Nelson/chromatic number of the plane https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadwiger%E2%80%93Nelson_problem [wikipedia.org] proof. As far as I'm aware (and I could be wrong) the technique it uses here is not in the literature, so this is a genuine construction of a new technique. Second is the Erdos Egyptian fraction bound, and for that one it looks like the techniques are about what I'd expect, but I'm definitely still digesting both of these. For the quasi-Riemann Hypothesis the striking thing is almost the opposite direction. It looks like the AI used standard complex analytic techniques to get the result. But many mathematicians have often thought for years that those techniques would not likely be strong enough to get this sort of result. I know less about the Unique Games Conjecture https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unique_games_conjecture [wikipedia.org] but having talked with some of the people there it looks like it took a somewhat standard set of ideas and then combined them with multiple just weird stuff and sort of took a hard left turn at one point for no clear reason and ended up at the result. The three I mentioned above have all also been looked at at this point by human mathematicians who have not found issues; that's likely true for others, but those three I'm aware at least of people doing so. Not all the claims have Lean code though; a bit under half. One of the non-formalized problems also has been withdrawn due to what essentially amounts to a sign error https://github.com/openai/math/blob/main/preprints/Algebraicity-of-Weil-classes-on-split-abelian-eightfolds-September-18-2026/paper.pdf [github.com]. It is likely others will be withdrawn also by the end, but I'd be surprised if more than 10 are. And even if everything single one without Lean code turned out to be wrong (which seems very unlikely), this would still be an amazing set of math. I commented elsewhere that if a human mathematician had made the quasi-RH result they'd be likely a shoe-in for the Fields Medal, and another mathematician replied saying "delete likely." Now a more editorial comment: There are legitimate concerns about what this is doing to mathematics. This sort of thing is very cool. But it also is part of a trend that may make it much harder to train young mathematicians or get them to exist at all. If the AIs are limited in how genuinely novel their ideas can be, then we may end up in a situation where we get a massive burst in math over the next few years, and then math stalls out because we don't have enough good really high caliber mathematicians (Not the mathematicians like me, but people like Serre, Tao, Scholze, Clausen,etc.) to come up with deeply new ideas that the AIs can build on. Trump Found a New Way to Do Family Separation Without the Backlash
2026-10-08

Trump Found a New Way to Do Family Separation Without the Backlash

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. Children are almost always treated with special deference in American law, because lawmakers understand and respect the significance of adolescence and the necessity of parental guidance. President Donald Trump has upended this long-standing, universal understanding in a particularly insidious way during his second term. For the first time in our nation’s history, tens of thousands of children are being separated from their parents, who, at the hands of Immigration and Customs Enforcement, are being violently arrested inside of the country, then detained, and quickly deported without their kids. These children are essentially left to fend for themselves or are placed with an unknown caregiver, which in some cases has led to horrific endings. Although this may sound awfully similar to Trump’s 2018 “zero-tolerance” policy, it is worse, because there is no explicit family separation policy this time around, making it harder to challenge in court. Instead, family separation has become just another devastating consequence of the president’s mass deportation agenda. Under past administrations, ICE policy held that any detainees who were parents should be reunited with their children before being deported, or agents must get confirmation that there is a designated caregiver in place for their children in the parents’ absence. Last year, upon Trump returning to the White House, ICE revised this policy to make it only applicable “when operationally feasible,” which gave agents all the leeway they needed to ignore the historical guidance in order to prioritize mass deportations. “This loosening of guidelines, combined with scaled-up immigration enforcement, has resulted in the detention of thousands of parents, many of whose minor children are U.S. citizens,” a recent analysis by the Migration Policy Institute concluded. Under Trump 2.0, the Department of Homeland Security no longer provides reliable data on its immigration enforcement, but the Brookings Institution estimates that as of April 2026 about 205,000 children have likely experienced a parental detention since Trump began his second term and about 145,000 of these children are U.S. citizens. Mothers in particular seem to be affected, with ProPublica finding that the Trump administration is deporting about four times as many moms of U.S. citizen children per day as the Biden administration did, most of whom have minor or no criminal histories at all. Stories of these children are playing out across the country, like in Minnesota, where three children have been separated from their father for nearly 10 months after he was yanked out of his car and violently arrested on his way to work. And in Alabama, where one woman has been caring for an old co-worker’s 2-year-old for nearly a year since ICE picked up the baby’s mother while she was on a grocery run. In Illinois, a college senior found himself the sole breadwinner of his family after ICE arrested and detained his uncle. Three sisters in San Francisco were among a lucky few who were reunited with their mother after she was detained for almost a year, leaving the eldest sibling to take care of the household while juggling college classes and navigating a complicated immigration system for her mother. Then there are the children who ICE arrests with one or both of their parents. Although the U.S. government has historically allowed families to remain in detention together, the Trump administration has quietly been separating children by giving their parents false promises that they’ll be able to stay in touch with them. In reality, as detailed by Mother Jones, oftentimes children are sent to the Office of Refugee Resettlement and reclassified as unaccompanied minors—they are not; the U.S. government knows their parents are in detention—and sent off to live with foster parents. ORR does not track these types of cases, so it’s unclear how many children are being reclassified, but Mother Jones estimated they now make up a “huge chunk” of ORR’s caseload. Under normal circumstances, unaccompanied minors are defined as children who arrive at a U.S. border alone, with no parent or guardian. Regardless of how the Trump administration chooses to manipulate immigration law, the National Immigration Law Center says parents and legal guardians who are detained by ICE have a legal right to make decisions about their minor child’s care. They are also allowed to see their children while in detention, upon written request, whether that’s in person or over a video or phone call. And if a parent faces deportation, they also have a legal right to make decisions about what happens to their children, which usually comes down to either allowing the child to remain in the U.S., regardless of the child’s immigration status, or to be deported alongside the parent. All of these decisions are supposed to happen with a lawyer, but even this is no longer happening, due to financial constraints, the administration’s attacks on legal aid for minors, and its extreme hostility toward immigrants, leaving parents with little to no recourse when ICE does not follow protocol. Children do not get to choose their parents’ immigration status, yet their families are being torn apart over it. The consequences are clear, with studies showing that children who lose a parent to sudden, forced deportation experience anxiety, anger, aggression, isolation, and depression. It also often leads to housing instability, academic withdrawal, and family dissolution. Yet, the president is unbothered by this; in fact, he’s doubled down on efforts to eliminate immigrant children from the U.S. altogether, evidenced by his birthright citizenship executive order. Despite the Supreme Court categorically rejecting it earlier this year, Trump has since introduced yet another executive order targeting birthright citizenship. The Trump administration’s assault on immigrants has been cruel at every turn, but what it’s doing to immigrant children could be its worst act by far. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction, and if you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! In this week’s episode of Amicus, Dahlia Lithwick discusses the botched execution of Tennessee inmate Christa Pike, which the Supreme Court allowed to move forward in a last-minute stay with zero explanation. Then, Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island joins to discuss SCOTUS’s new term and the climate case Suncor v. Boulder. In the Amicus bonus episode, Dahlia talks about the latest attack on abortion rights with Jay Willis, the editor in chief of Balls and Strikes. Blue states are fighting to protect their abortion laws as red states claim those laws violate their legal authority, with the Supreme Court yet to declare whether it will take up their case. Also on the agenda is the president’s third-country removal policy and the growing pains of SCOTUSblog. The midterm elections are right around the corner, so what could Trump do to try to subvert U.S. democracy? Richard Hasen, professor of law at UCLA, outlines five levers the president could pull and how states, courts, and the people could respond. On Monday, the Supreme Court held oral arguments in Suncor Energy v. Boulder County, and Madiba K. Dennie, attorney and deputy editor at Balls and Strikes, explains what went down. As Colorado attempted to argue it should be able to sue oil and gas companies for fueling climate change, conservative Justices Brett Kavanaugh and Amy Coney Barrett seemed to openly worry about the economic prospects of oil and gas companies. LGBTQ+ rights are very much on the Supreme Court’s docket this term, and Hila Keren, professor of law at Southwestern Law School, explains what’s at stake. In International Partners for Ethical Care Inc. v. Ferguson, the focus is on a Washington state law that protects access to safe state-licensed youth shelters and subsidized mental healthcare counseling. In Trump v. Kingdom, the administration is asking to lift an injunction that’s preventing it from implementing policies that deprive trans prisoners of healthcare. American Alliances Can Outlast Trump. But They Might Not Survive What Comes After Him.
2026-10-08

American Alliances Can Outlast Trump. But They Might Not Survive What Comes After Him.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. “Trump’s lukewarm support for NATO has led some European allies to question if the US would come to their aid if Russia were to attack them,” the Guardian reports. “In Europe, Asia, and the Middle East, governments are assessing shifting US priorities and in some cases seeking alternative sources of leadership and partnership in the belief that America has stepped back,” CNN assesses. “Why is Trump criticizing key allies to U.S. security?” a PBS News Hour segment wonders, incredulity implied. “Canadians have had enough,” the New York Times concludes after Trump berated the country’s prime minister on social media and pledged to hit it with yet more tariffs. The onslaught has veteran diplomats, foreign-policy gurus, and members of both parties bemoaning that Donald Trump is irreversibly squandering America’s long-standing partnerships and emboldening its foes. As one veteran of the George W. Bush and Obama administrations put it, “This is how the liberal international order dies.” Except all those stories are from Trump’s first term. And what happened after? Trump lost the 2020 election, and U.S. alliances rebounded. Under the Biden administration, Western nations rallied to Ukraine’s defense, and NATO expanded. South Korea and Japan shelved decades of historical enmity to present China with a united front. The U.S., U.K., and Australia signed a maritime security pact known as AUKUS. The so-called Quad alliance of Australia, India, the U.S., and Japan knitted closer together. Trump is now back in the White House, his presence once again conjuring nearly identical fears of imminent alliance collapse. “The American Age Is Over,” an Atlantic headline put it last month, and not without some justification. The story appeared as Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney was traveling to Strasbourg, France, to explain why he wants to make Canada an “associate member” of the European Union, a clear effort to seek new partnerships as the U.S. goes rogue. “We are pursuing resilience so that no one can control our open markets, impair our sovereignty, threaten our territorial integrity, or undermine our freedoms, our democracies, our rule of law,” Carney said—not-so-subtle references to Trump’s efforts to swamp Canada with tariffs and bully it into becoming the 51st U.S. state. Other global leaders have similarly looked to forge more reliable friendships elsewhere, and the reason for their concerns was on display at the United Nations General Assembly in New York last month. “To the victor belong the spoils,” Trump told the delegates regarding the U.S. intervention in Venezuela that ousted the South American nation’s leader this year and effectively seized much of its oil reserves, a stark summation of America’s rapacious new global identity. So is this time any different? Will Trump’s bellicose and chaotic second term turn the U.S. into a global pariah, rearranging global alliances in a lasting way? Or will the inevitable end of his presidency (only 835 days to go!) once again uncork a surge of goodwill among American friends? Some think the experience of Trump’s first term makes the pessimistic case for the future of American alliances look shortsighted. And amid the doomsaying, streaks of contrarian optimism are starting to emerge. The most well-thought-out example is a Foreign Affairs essay published in August by Kurt M. Campbell and Rush Doshi, who served on the National Security Council during the Biden administration. The way Campbell and Doshi see things, America’s alliances have already survived far worse than Trump. Since World War II, the challenges have included diplomatic crises, financial crises, energy shocks, and the catastrophes of Vietnam, Afghanistan, and Iraq. “The lesson of history is that alliance systems often prove more resilient in retrospect than contemporary observers expect at the time,” they write. “Crises repeatedly generate predictions of collapse, but alliances adapt precisely because the underlying incentives remain compelling.” What are those incentives? One of them is aligned interests. We often shorthand alliances as friendships, but they’re usually built less on comity and more on mutual necessity. And as Campell and Doshi argue, the interests of America and its long-standing allies are more conjoined today than they have been in decades. Russian aggression and Chinese aspirations for economic dominance have ripped off the mask, presenting a shared challenge that only unity can meet. The other factor, Campell and Doshi say, is interdependence in the form of growing economic, technological, and military ties to counterbalance China’s industrial might. Rays of hope are always welcome in this dark era, but there are at least three reasons to think the authors’ case—and the optimistic read on the future of American alliances writ large—has some holes. Campbell and Doshi may be right that some of the ways Trump is subverting American alliances “owe more to presidential personality than to durable new politics.” But this president has repeatedly transmuted other quirks of his personality (his refusal to concede the 2020 election, his obsession with building a ballroom at the White House, and more) into Republican Party orthodoxy. It’s true that many GOP members of Congress remain supportive of NATO, critical of Russia, and leery of China. But most of the voters who elect those lawmakers are thoroughly Trumpified. The president has also purged Republican officials who object to his “America First” approach and co-opted some of the party’s older foreign-policy hands. As a Bloomberg Businessweek cover story about Secretary of State Marco Rubio recently put it, Rubio “is executing the president’s wishes around the world, even when they contradict his past beliefs.” As a senator, Rubio successfully helped pass a bill that prevents American presidents from unilaterally pulling out of NATO. But after accusing several allies of impeding the war in Iran by blocking access to military bases on their soil last month, Rubio seemed to question why NATO exists at all. “If we can’t use our base when we have a contingency, what’s the point of having the base and, frankly, the point of the alliance?” he said on Fox News. For U.S. allies watching from the outside, one Trump term may have been an aberration, but two looks like a trend. Yes, Trump is unpopular with Americans right now. But he was almost as disliked at the end of his first term before going on to win a second, and there’s little reason to think he’ll voluntarily cede his kingmaker status within the GOP even after leaving office. Given those conditions, it seems more likely than not that the next Republican presidential nominee will take after Trump’s pro-tariff, anti-NATO policies more than he or she will resemble the pro-alliance, anti-Russia GOP of yore. And because our politics tends to oscillate between the two dominant parties, the rest of the globe should expect someone of Trump’s worldview to hold power roughly half the time, at least for the foreseeable future. That’s some shaky ground on which to repair bruised friendships. Trump’s presidencies have also changed Democrats. His return undermined Joe Biden’s efforts to rehabilitate and stabilize U.S. alliances. (“America is back,” Biden frequently told world leaders, to which some of them—at least in his telling—presciently responded “But for how long?”) The damage Trump has done to American institutions, meanwhile, has some of the ambitious Democrats who hope to succeed him in the White House thinking closer to home—about economic investment, Supreme Court reform, and other domestic policies rather than about America’s place in the world. Many progressives are skeptical of flexing American military power abroad and have sometimes cast U.S. efforts to confront Russia and China as jingoistic spasms or sops to the defense industry. That’s a recipe for continuing to let alliances stagnate regardless of which party wins in 2028. A second reason to doubt Campbell and Doshi’s case is that some of America’s allies are already benefiting from the president’s shake-up of the status quo. Nondemocracies, like Saudi Arabia and Qatar, have turned the president’s more transactional approach to geopolitics—not to mention his and his family’s susceptibility to corruption—to their advantage. Inside other allied countries, rising political factions share Trump’s right-wing, xenophobic, and isolationist orientation. His first election helped uncork a boomlet of right-wing populism, and his second has boosted it further. Last month, the far-right Alliance for Germany party won a closely watched state election in a landslide. Reform UK, led by Trump ally Nigel Farage, has topped surveys of the next national election for much of the past year. Marine Le Pen, an EU skeptic who leads France’s far-right National Rally party, is ahead in polls of next year’s presidential race. Flávio Bolsonaro, a right-wing Trump ally and the son of a jailed former president who attempted a coup in the mold of Jan. 6 back in 2023, topped the first round of Brazil’s presidential election this week and is favored to win the runoff later this month. That means Trump doesn’t have to turn the U.S. into a pariah for its historical alliances to lose ground to Russia and China; he could instead transform those alliances into something closer to the countries they purport to oppose. Campbell and Doshi aren’t Pollyannas. They appreciate that some of Trump’s second-term spasms, like threatening to annex Greenland by force, have been more extreme than anything he did in his first. Still, they may be underrating just how badly another Trump presidency—especially one defined by tariffs and a war that are raising prices worldwide—is poisoning America’s international reputation. Campbell and Doshi take comfort in the fact that many European citizens tell pollsters that they expect their countries’ relations with the U.S. to improve once Trump leaves office. But the authors don’t reckon with polls showing that people in dozens of countries now view China more positively than they view the U.S., a sharp reversal even from Trump’s first term. According to a Pew Research Center survey this year, nations that now feel warmer about China include Australia, Canada, France, Germany, Greece, Italy, the Netherlands, Pakistan, Spain, and Sweden—all of which are nominally U.S. allies. Growing pessimism with the U.S. may also be helping collapse the underpinnings of American dominance to a degree that Trump’s first term never did. As the New York Times reported last month, even friendly countries are reducing their holdings of U.S. Treasury bonds amid soaring yields. The dollar’s share of currency reserves held by foreign governments, already on the decline, dropped further after Trump retook office. And the president’s efforts to fire Federal Reserve officials, jawbone companies, and turn the law against his political opponents have made some question whether the U.S. is still a safe investment. Those jitters are undermining the dollar’s 82-year run as the world’s reserve currency, threatening a sea change that would be hard for even the most internationalist of Trump successors to reverse. In Campbell and Doshi’s telling, alliances are like gravity: Other Western leaders may complain about Trump now, but they’ll ultimately get over it because doing so is in their countries’ best interest. Once Trump passes from the scene, the need to put the past aside and confront a militarily strong, technologically advanced, and territorially ambitious China will become “increasingly clear to policymakers in allied capitals, just as it should become clear to post-Trump American leaders,” Campbell and Doshi write. But just because what’s in one’s own self-interest should become clear doesn’t mean it will—or that policymakers in the U.S. and elsewhere will heed it. After all, if the benefits of strong alliances were self-evident to American voters or to Trump, we wouldn’t be in this position in the first place. Rather than clarity, the legacy of Trump’s second term could instead be a sort of global muddle. In that scenario, Canada, Europe, and parts of Asia would meaningfully distance themselves from the U.S. while also seeking workable relationships with China or Russia. In fact, some of America’s long-standing allies have already started straddling. Earlier this year, Carney traveled to Beijing to inaugurate a “new strategic partnership” that saw Canada cut tariffs on Chinese electric cars in exchange for lower tariffs on Canadian agricultural goods. If allied countries can’t rely on the U.S. to serve as a predictable counterweight to China, detente with Beijing might be preferable to confrontation—even as they surely understand that it poses more of a threat to free markets and democracy than even Trump does. Trump still has two years left in office, which means things could get worse for America’s transatlantic allies before they get better. Last month, he called Carney’s pivot to Europe a potential “hostile act” that could invite “very heavy tariffs on Europe.” It’s true that some transatlantic leaders share Campbell and Doshi’s sunny outlook. Finnish President Alexander Stubb, whose country joined NATO during the Biden administration, “is betting that the alliance will adapt and survive” in the years to come, the Washington Post reported this week. But that kind of optimism can quickly start to sound more like whistling past the graveyard. Stubb, whose friendship with Trump grew out of a shared affinity for golf, is banking in part on his “good relationship” with Jared Kushner to help negotiate an end to Russia’s war in Ukraine. But as the Times reported last week, Kushner’s involvement may not be totally above board. U.S. talks with Russia now include a multibillion-dollar oil deal that could enrich Middle Eastern fat cats who have previously invested in Trump family projects, including the president’s cryptocurrency company. And despite his upbeat perspective, even Stubb acknowledges that Europe needs to forge new partnerships in “a world of disorder.” The ship of state is seeking safety in a storm. That kind of hedging is the danger of making friends mad. It’s also the hidden lesson of the history Campbell and Doshi relate in their essay. They’re correct that America’s alliances have weathered struggles before. But that’s because the bonds of friendship are hard enough to maintain when the president representing us on the world stage is rational. When he isn’t, all bets are off. A Progressive Democrat Tried to Woo MAHA Republicans. The Early Response Is Revealing.
2026-10-07

A Progressive Democrat Tried to Woo MAHA Republicans. The Early Response Is Revealing.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Locked in a tight Senate race in Michigan, progressive Democrat Abdul El-Sayed is vying for every vote he can get—and even some he probably can’t. El-Sayed is reaching out to the “Make America Healthy Again” movement, a loosely affiliated group of voters, many of them part of Donald Trump’s 2024 coalition, best known for their opposition to vaccines, as well as their concern about chemicals in our food, water, and air. In a bid for their support, the Michigan Democrat is pushing what his campaign calls the “Building a Healthier America” agenda, which focuses on having fewer chemicals and toxins in food and crops, healthier lunches, more funding for public health programs, breaking up “corporate concentration in agriculture,” and purging Michigan’s drinking water of microplastics. The agenda is big on healthier lunches in schools, and the campaign is calling for public policy to “integrate healthy food into how Michigan delivers healthcare.” El-Sayed’s effort is representative of a broader push among Democrats to make inroads into a MAHA movement that Trump largely captured by co-opting its standard-bearer. The movement’s unofficial leader is Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who ran for president in 2024 before dropping out and endorsing Trump. Not coincidentally, Trump subsequently picked Kennedy to be his Health and Human Services secretary. Atop HHS, Kennedy has taken a much more adversarial stance toward vaccines and vaccine requirements, a reflection of his longtime push to connect vaccines for children with health issues, including autism. (To be clear, Kennedy is trafficking in conspiracy theories and is on the wrong side of science. Vaccines are safe and effective in preventing diseases that once made child mortality catastrophically higher than it is today.) But the MAHA-MAGA coalition is not without its points of friction, particularly when it comes to agriculture and pesticide companies with deep pockets and close ties to lawmakers in both parties—but particularly farm-state Republicans. MAHA had hoped for more restrictive policies on pesticides and a crackdown on industrialized agriculture, but they’ve come away disappointed. In February, Trump signed an executive order promoting the production of a weed-killing chemical that MAHA had targeted, leaving many in the movement disillusioned. They were further disappointed when Kennedy came out in support of the order in one of the more obvious fall-on-your-pesticide-laden-sword moments of recent political history. Democrats are hoping to exploit that rift. “It’s smart for Abdul to appeal to MAHA voters in Michigan, some of whom are definitely persuadable given President Trump’s record on what he told MAHA he would do in office versus what he’s done,” a veteran Michigan political strategist said. Nabbing even a few MAHA voters could go a long way. El-Sayed is running against Republican Mike Rogers, and most recent polls put the Democrat narrowly ahead. There’s some evidence of crossover appeal. “We are very interested in any candidate who is taking the issue of pesticides, toxins in our environment, and health seriously,” said Zen Honeycutt, who identifies as part of the MAHA movement and is the founding executive director of Moms Across America, a prominent group within the broader MAHA movement. There is an enormous sticking point to all of this: vaccines—and, more specifically, vaccine mandates. “I understand that [El-Sayed] believes in vaccines, and that’s fine with most of us as long as he doesn’t push his beliefs on us,” Honeycutt said. “Meaning, as long as, as an elected official, he does not support vaccine mandates that prevent our children from going to school or camp or church or anything of the like.” I asked El-Sayed’s campaign where the candidate stands. The campaign pointed to El-Sayed telling CBS that he would “always be evidence-driven when it comes to vaccines … but the key thing is that you want to know what you’re feeding your kids and what you’re putting in the body is pure. And RFK Jr. and Donald Trump and Mike Rogers have failed that in droves.” El-Sayed added that he looked forward to “listening and learning” and that “where there might be disagreements on vaccine policy, I think we all agree that Big Pharma is a real problem.” That’s a notable nonanswer on whether he supports vaccine mandates, which are in place in the overwhelmingly majority of schools nationwide. (It turns out that most parents don’t want their kids sharing toys with peers who are, due to their parents’ choices, at elevated risk for carrying deadly diseases.) Basically, the candidate is saying that he’ll hear MAHA out on vaccines, but his policy preferences on the issue aren’t going to align with theirs. And for Honeycutt and her fellow ideological travelers, that sure seems like a dealbreaker. “I guarantee you our moms will be looking into his stance on vaccines,” she said of El-Sayed. “If he at all hints at being in favor of vaccine mandates, that will cancel out his appeal as far as pesticides and making America healthy again.” MAHA is an expansive movement, and Honeycutt doesn’t speak for all of it. But it’s difficult to imagine many MAHA voters abandoning their signature issue for an El-Sayed vote. That’s not a dismissal of the outreach attempt so much as a reflection of where the MAHA movement is in reality. Democrats would have a decent shot at voters who equally weighted vaccines with, say, organic food or clean-water protections, but that voter is more often aspirational than corporeal. Because on vaccines, the issue on which MAHA voters are most decisively in conflict with the scientific consensus, they are the most dug in. And no number of white papers, roundtables, or outreach events is going to change that. The bulk of El-Sayed’s efforts, however, still focus on turning out Democrats’ base. On Monday, the campaign said that later this month the Senate contender would be campaigning alongside former President Barack Obama. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Fighting GenAI with GenAI: The New Email Security Landscape
2026-10-08

Fighting GenAI with GenAI: The New Email Security Landscape

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED FEATURE: Old attack, new protections The use of phishing emails as a means of stealing information or implanting damaging malware dates back to the mid-1990s. Although almost as old as the Internet, it remains the instrument of choice for threat actors wanting to pose as trusted organizations or individuals for the purposes of corporate infiltration. In fact its use is on the rise: according to the most recent figures from the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), some 26 percent of all cybercrime complaints filed with them are now phishing-related. The bad news doesn’t stop there. Phishing is mutating in alarming new ways, raising all sorts of difficult questions for defenders and placing email security at a tricky inflection point. AI has for years been a useful tool in the hands of the cybercriminal. But the advent of Generative AI (GenAI) is proving a cyber game changer, transforming phishing from a widespread but easily identifiable nuisance into a lethal precision instrument. Aspiring phishers have historically been hindered by various constraints. Their attempts at chummy authenticity have often been undermined by easy-to-spot mistakes and amateurish formatting – blemishes that traditional email security is good at picking up. Personalised attacks have also been hard to launch at any kind of scale. GenAI sweeps all technical, linguistic, and operational restrictions aside by automating sophistication. Attackers no longer have to choose between individually targeted “spear phishing” which takes much effort to coordinate, and large-scale attacks that lack finesse. Now, with minimal expertise and at low cost, they can hit thousands of victims with minutely tailored phishes at the press of a button. Today’s Large Language Models (LLMs) generate polished and contextual text in any language, backing it with realistic brand graphics and convincing web addresses. At a stroke, Gen AI has democratized this type of crime, putting it in reach of anyone capable of the most elementary research. “Thanks to AI, the historic signals that exposed a phishing email – poor grammar, misspelt words – are now ironed out and replaced with perfect language,” notes Dave Baggett, SVP Cybersecurity with software developer Kaseya. “These emails can be highly targeted with the help of AI. You can create an email that pinpoints, say, a pharma company by using authentic terminology. It’s an incredibly powerful tool for the bad guys.” And let’s not forget that where this new AI-enabled phishing is successful, it is not just a single unwary employee whose data is at risk. Once past the perimeter, a bad actor can get to work burrowing into the cloud and SaaS platforms that now underpin global commerce and communications. All in all, it’s easy to understand how losses from phishing attacks have gone up 274 percent in the last couple of years, according to the FBI. This new email security landscape is an alarming development for corporate IT bosses, and also for MSPs who must be super wary on behalf of customers who don’t have the expertise to pick up attacks at this pitch of sophistication on their own. It’s not all bad news on the email security front line. As threat actors have been perfecting their use of GenAI, defenders have been developing AI-driven counter solutions in parallel. Where old school protection relied on spotting the kind of anomalies that GenAI has now ironed out, newer solutions are geared more towards contextual analysis. They are not so much trying to spot clumsy typos and dodgy attachments as model an attacker’s intent before damage is done. Today defenders can use AI to analyze subtle patterns that are in tune with the layered structure of modern phishing campaigns. The aim is to determine whether a message aligns with expected behavioral patterns. This level of finesse is made necessary by the insidious nature of modern phishing which means that technically clean email messages, in other words ones that are free of malware-loaded attachments or booby-trapped links, can still contain malicious potential. Modern cybercriminals often bypass automated security filters by the simple means of exploiting human psychology and trusted infrastructure. Social engineering, supercharged by GenAI, can be a sharper sword than a malware payload. A phish will often seek to impersonate a boss or a colleague using a plain text email. Attackers can set GenAI to scour LinkedIn, looking for people who have just started new jobs. Green employees are more vulnerable to a phish that looks like an email from a new superior they haven’t got to know yet. Once trust is established, what will typically follow is a demand for, say, an urgent wire transfer, or a casual request for payroll details or passwords. Attackers can develop a relationship over multiple emails to “groom” the victim before striking. Traditional filters see nothing suspicious. Spotting this sort of attack demands a smarter grade of tool. “Since attackers are relying on manipulation of fallible individuals, security must now support them at the moment where wrong decisions can be made,” says Baggett. “Good email security will replace generic warnings with easily digestible context about why a message might be risky and advice about the need for further verification.” Where once an inbox was a passive delivery channel, says Baggett, it is now an active layer of risk mitigation, tooled up to stamp out a phishing attempt before it escalates into account compromise and devastating financial loss. The good guys are fighting back. But as Baggett points out, there remains a huge asymmetry between bad actor and defender: “Attackers can use LLMs, basically for free, to create perfect phishing templates,” he says. “On the other hand, if defenders took every inbound email and put it through a frontier LLM model, that’s about 100x too expensive. We just can’t use the same tools at the criminals.” One option for defenders is to use smaller, more specialized models, distilled from larger ones. A compact model could be tuned, for example, to the sentiment or meaning of each phrase in an email, and set to pick up a threatening tone or a favor being asked. Where chunky LLMs just cost too much, another choice might be to run a subset of the overall inbound mail through a big model: “Admins can be given a way to run particular mails through a frontier LLM,” says Baggett. “That model will have been trained to understand certain critical aspects of email security.” Better use could be made of pre-LLM tools such as computer vision, which can help process, analyze and understand visual data like digital images and videos. Other ancillary tools that could enrich the defensive mix include sender analysis to look into the origin and authenticity of an email message, and linked-content inspection that can examine an email in depth without triggering any embedded threats. There are some reasons to hope for a safer future as AI technology evolves. As inference becomes smarter and models get better, it should become possible to run a higher percentage of emails through a large scale LLM, believes Baggett. This, however, may not be achievable for some years. There’s also the possibility of using LLMs not just to spot potentially malicious emails but also to analyze the settings and admin controls of existing security tools. In this way, the knowledge required to use the tools to best effect becomes much less. “Security tools can be complex and feature hundreds of settings,” points out Baggett. “Some require expertise that our customers don’t have. There are a number of things we believe tools should be doing to help defenders, for example reduce alert fatigue.” The criminal fraternity may, of course, also find ways to harness better tools for new purposes. At the moment, most attacks are conceived and designed by humans. But ultimately, this job may be taken over by agentic models able to figure out new tactics for themselves. Machines may soon be able to plan and supervise attacks at scale. Luckily defenders don’t have to sit back and be at the mercy of future developments. There are some great tools available today that help them even up the odds and cut the complexity from checking email. INKY Smart Insights, for example, uses GenAI to reduce a lengthy manual email investigation to a few seconds of automated triage. For the purposes of hard-pressed administrators it can turn a mess of technical email evidence into a plain-language verdict and enable the creation of cogent reports. Along with Kaseya Intelligence it can support decision-making across the broader security environment. “Smart Insights will identify problems and give a narrative explanation of its reasoning,” enthuses Baggett. “It will do that in a way that’s not overly technical, making the tool valuable to someone who doesn’t have expertise in email security.” The hard-pressed MSP, for example, can leverage Smart Insights to provide key information for their customers in an abbreviated and digestible format. In short, it allows them to enjoy the sophistication of LLMs but without adding hugely to their overheads. The war on GenAI-powered phishing is far from won. But so long as defenders can understand the potential of the smart tools at their disposal, they have a fighting chance of keeping the bad guys at bay and essential channels of communication safe. Margaret Hamilton, software pioneer who helped put humans on the Moon, dies at 90
2026-10-08

Margaret Hamilton, software pioneer who helped put humans on the Moon, dies at 90

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Her team's code kept Apollo 11's landing on course when the guidance computer became overloaded "Forget It" was the name of a small computer program never thought to be needed, which will nonetheless go down in history along with its author, Margaret Hamilton, the American software engineer who died on September 30, aged 90. Hamilton led development of the onboard flight software for Apollo's command and lunar modules, helping astronauts reach the Moon in 1969. Among her early jobs after joining the MIT Instrumentation Laboratory in 1965 was to write the software for the Apollo abort procedure. "Everybody said that was never going to happen, [so they said] 'Oh well, good. We'll give this one to Margaret because she's a beginner,'" she told the Computer History Museum. However, an unmanned flight did abort, raising Hamilton's standing within the team. "It went to [run] this program I had written, which I named Forget It. All of a sudden, I became an overnight expert," she said. Hamilton was born in Paoli, Indiana. Moving with her family to Michigan, she graduated from high school in 1954. She studied mathematics at the University of Michigan and Earlham College, where she graduated in 1958 with a minor in philosophy. She planned to pursue graduate studies and an academic career, but decided to go to Boston in 1959 with her husband, who was studying law at Harvard. She got a job at MIT with Edward Lorenz – who went on to pioneer chaos theory – and programmed the LGP-30 and DEC's PDP-1 computers for weather forecasting. She worked on other projects at MIT, including aerospace systems, before hearing of the Apollo missions in 1965. She immediately applied to join the MIT team supporting them. She initially wrote software for uncrewed Apollo missions, later leading development of flight software for the crewed program. The team's software played a crucial role in the first lunar landing as the Apollo 11 module approached the Moon's surface in 1969. During the final minutes of descent, the guidance computer raised several unexpected alarms, leading the astronauts to question whether it was safe to land. The alarms showed "executive overflows," meaning the system could not complete all its tasks. Hamilton had also pressed for safeguards against operator error. Her daughter, four years old at the time, crashed a command-module simulation by activating a pre-launch program during a simulated flight. Hamilton proposed protection against that mistake, but was initially overruled because astronauts were considered unlikely to make it. An astronaut subsequently did so during Apollo 8, prompting changes to the software. For Apollo 11, the software's restart protection allowed essential landing tasks to continue despite the overload. Computer engineer Jack Garman advised guidance officer Steve Bales that the alarms did not require an abort. "We're go on that alarm," Bales confirmed. The crew made it safely to the Moon's surface and later returned to Earth. The overload was traced to unwanted inputs from the rendezvous radar system. While working on the Apollo missions, Hamilton began to call herself a "software engineer" because writing code was not taken as seriously as other engineering disciplines. The term is now standard. She also features in an iconic photograph, standing beside a stack of her team's Apollo software listings almost as tall as she was. Following the Apollo missions, she founded and co-founded a number of technology companies focused on fault-tolerant software. She created the Development Before the Fact (DBTF) methodology to prevent errors early on, along with the Universal Systems Language (USL) for systems design. In 2016, President Barack Obama awarded her the Presidential Medal of Freedom. Computing pioneer Grace Hopper received the same honor posthumously. The White House described Hamilton as a mathematician and computer scientist who contributed to concepts of asynchronous software, priority scheduling and priority displays, and human-in-the-loop decision capability, which set the foundation for modern, ultra-reliable software design and engineering. Hamilton's legacy is a reminder to design for the mistakes users supposedly won't make – and the failures no one expects. She is survived by her daughter, a son-in-law, two grandsons, four great-grandchildren, and three siblings, according to MIT. ® Credential-stealing malware detected within minutes of npm release, but impact remains unknown Researchers corroborate parts of Silent Ransom Group dump detailing crypto payouts, cash brokers, and recruitment PARTNER CONTENT: VergeIO says an exit is also an opportunity Gartner predicts more than half will fold by 2030, even as businesses begin testing the technology's usefulness SPONSORED FEATURE: Old attack, new protections But don't worry, it's totally safe because the agents and models will (probably) be running locally. Rust upstart reveals 'reimplementation' of Microsoft Office – with Claude on coding duty
2026-10-08

Rust upstart reveals 'reimplementation' of Microsoft Office – with Claude on coding duty

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
ArtCraft adds Word, Excel, and PowerPoint lookalikes to its open source lineup, but they're not ready for production ArtCraft has added alternatives to Microsoft Word, Excel, and PowerPoint to its growing collection of Rust applications, which it describes as "clean-room reimplementations" of proprietary software. The projects lean heavily on Claude for development and remain unfinished, but our first look at its Word alternative, WordCraft, suggests they are worth watching. The company's GitHub repositories also include projects modeled on Photoshop, Illustrator, and Premiere Pro. We fired up WordCraft on an Arm-based Surface and it worked pretty well. WordCraft resembles Microsoft's application, although its buttons, icons, and other imagery differ. "The spelling dictionary and hyphenation come from Grady Ward's public-domain Moby Hyphenator II word list," ArtCraft says, and the application is dual-licensed under MIT or Apache 2.0. The company is at pains to point out that WordCraft is at the pre-alpha stage and estimates that it is at 62 percent of real feature parity, although "an alpha for everyday writing is close." We would keep it away from production workloads for now. Its roadmap says testing DOCX compatibility against a collection of real-world files has not begun, and charts, SmartArt, and embedded OLE objects are dropped. The Document Foundation, which develops LibreOffice, declined to comment. Microsoft acknowledged our inquiry but offered no further response. Adobe also declined to comment on the projects targeting its creative applications. Neil Brown of decoded.legal told The Register that the legal position would depend on the jurisdiction, but suggested that "those doing the 'reimplementations' may well argue that, since they have implemented the underlying functionality independently (i.e. without access to the source code of the software that they are reimplementing), their overall reimplementation is non-infringing." That said, "the legal situation is always going to be interesting, and quite possibly litigated, when the party most likely to feel aggrieved has deep pockets and lots of lawyers, and probably quite a lot to lose." ArtCraft says WordCraft was built from public specifications and observation, using original or openly licensed assets. Its ambitions now extend from creative software to office applications, but whether the projects can deliver reliable replacements remains to be seen. CrowdStrike finds possible bank hacker's CV among exposed AI logs

CrowdStrike finds possible bank hacker's CV among exposed AI logs

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Suspected Chinese speaker used Claude Code and agentic pentesting tool ARTEX in attacks on South Korean lenders CrowdStrike researchers investigating attacks on South Korean financial institutions found exposed AI session logs containing operational details and a resume-writing request that may identify the attacker. In a report published Wednesday, analyst Ashley Campion said the prompt named "YY," listed a Chinese university and a location in Guangdong, and gave conflicting age information: 26, but initially a birth date in September 2007. CrowdStrike considers the details likely to belong to the attacker but says it cannot definitively establish that connection. The attacks affected at least five lenders, including Shinhan Bank, KB Kookmin Bank, Hana Bank, Yegaram Savings Bank, and BNK Busan Bank, according to The Korea Times. In one case, the attackers allegedly breached a loan progress inquiry service and in another, they gained access to a mobile work-support system. The researchers found references to YY in AI sessions associated with activity involving ARTEX, a recently released open source penetration-testing tool developed in China and used in the attacks alongside Claude Code. Researchers examined an exposed directory on a server associated with the attacks. A Chinese-language instruction file led them to another server in Hong Kong, where they found session histories, configuration files, and AI memory files documenting the targeting. The resume prompt included a Telegram username that also appeared in activity targeting a possible Chinese payment platform and in Claude Code sessions seeking vulnerabilities in a Telegram-based NFT gift marketplace, CrowdStrike said. "The use of agentic AI tooling alongside traditional offensive capabilities highlights the continued evolution observed by CrowdStrike in adversarial tradecraft," Campion said. "This activity demonstrates how AI tooling can enable a financially motivated threat actor to conduct multiple intrusions within a short time span. CrowdStrike Intelligence assesses that adversaries will likely continue to experiment with implementing AI tooling in their operations to enhance their operational tempo and capabilities." Police are investigating whether an individual or an organized group carried out the attacks. Shinhan Bank reported that about 25,000 customers were affected, while KB Kookmin and Hana reported 119 and 89 respectively.
