MIT Technology Review
Weight-loss drugs show signs of slowing biological aging, say drugmakers
2026-10-06

Weight-loss drugs show signs of slowing biological aging, say drugmakers

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-06

Visit Original Website
Popular weight-loss drugs may do more than help people shed pounds. They might also melt away the years. Drug giants Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk say patients taking their drugs age less quickly, according to readouts from molecular “aging clocks.” Such clocks assess a person’s biological age by looking at changes to DNA that accumulate with time, or, in newer versions, by tracking levels of key proteins. Both companies found that overweight or diabetic patients taking the drugs, called GLP-1s, had reduced biological age compared to those taking a placebo, although the difference varied widely, depending on which type of clock was used, and what organ was tested. Overall, the difference was around “two to three years,” according to Nikolaj Roed, a global project leader at Novo, who says the company has been seeing “improved biological age in our patients across trials and across different tissues.” The findings add to wide speculation among scientists that GLP-1 drugs, as they are known, are acting on basic causes of aging and might be a true longevity treatment. “Two years is a pretty strong effect, in my book,” says Steve Horvath, a professor at the University of California, Los Angeles, who’s credited with inventing aging clocks. Horvath says the emerging data could provide “evidence that these GLP-1 drugs are actually what is known as geroprotectors, medications that slow or possibly even reverse biologic aging.” The companies shared their findings over the weekend during Aging Research & Drug Discovery, a conference devoted to seeking scientific remedies for old age. While that quest has not yet produced any clear-cut success, some scientists now think Novo’s drug semaglutide (sold under the names Ozempic and Wegovy) is coming close. “In an unhealthy population, I do think it’s an anti-aging drug,” says Vadim Gladyshev, a Harvard biologist who assisted Novo with its molecular measurements. “But in a healthy population, no one knows.” The drugs cause weight loss by stimulating a receptor, GLP-1, that tells your brain you’re not hungry. Yet real-world studies have shown much wider benefit. The drugs improve kidney function, reduce blood pressure, and even sharply cut the overall chance of death. “If the question is,‘Can semaglutide reach several diseases relevant to health span and aging?’ we know we can say the answer is yes,” said Alejandro Aguayo-Orozco, a senior scientific director at Novo, the Danish drug giant, during the meeting. The next question to answer, he said, is whether such effects are accompanied by changes to molecular measures of biological aging: “When you intervene with semaglutide, does it actually move the clocks in any direction? And the answer is yes.” The company found that, over time, the drugs cause a wide slowdown in aging clocks—as much as 4 years in the case of a clock that looks at heart proteins. “I think it’s pretty clear that the organ age is shifting,” said Aguayo-Orozco. The studies are also a huge boost for the science of aging clocks. Although these measures reflect a person’s age, it’s been uncertain if they are useful as true biomarkers. Now clock makers have evidence  that their readouts show a drop in age when people take drugs with broad, well-demonstrated benefits. “The clock people have been pushing for a decade to get this kind of study done,” says Yuge Ji, a biologist who previously worked in the field and now runs a startup, Reflector Bio. “It’s huge for them.” As part of its study, Novo took blood draws from 10,052 people, half on the drug and half on a placebo. Their blood, collected at the start of the study as well as months later, was then measured with “proteomic clocks,” which use levels of key proteins to predict a person’s age and risk of dying. Scientists at Eli Lilly performed similar research on their GLP-1 drug, tirzepatide, using so-called “epigenetic” clocks that assess age by counting accumulated changes to DNA. Lilly’s study was smaller, but also found that for the most part, molecular time moved more slowly for people on the drug. “All the clocks are telling a consistent story that we’re seeing a reduction in age,” Kevin Duffin, vice president for aging research at Lilly, said during the conference. “It’s not like we’re going to reverse age by 30 years or something, but it’s a significant reduction.” The molecules have become the best selling drugs in the world,, with Lilly’s tirzepatide, sold as Mounjaro for diabetes and Zepbound for obesity, topping the list with more than $36 billion in revenue to the company last year. Novo’s semaglutide, also sold under more than one name, was a close second. Alex Zhavoronkov, the founder of Insilico Medicine, and the organizer of the Boston meeting, says that extending lives by even one year across the world’s population would be equal to tens of millions of lifetimes. Last month Zhavoronkov showed that one of his company’s drugs, for a lung disease, also reversed the signals from aging clocks. That drug is experimental, but Zhavoronkov has sought to promote the idea that humanity is entering a new era of longevity medicines. He also disclosed during the event that he has been “microdosing” the available weight-loss drugs, even though he is not overweight. “I am on tirzepatide and I am an equal-opportunity injector of semaglutide as well,” Zhavoronkov said. It was part of an effort to directly raise the question of whether the first mass-market anti-aging remedy is already here. “Do you think a reasonable person should start taking semaglutide?” he asked Aguayo-Orozco, the Novo scientist, in front of a crowded audience. “I am not a physician. I cannot answer that question,” Aguayo-Orozco replied. Several people cautioned that GLP-1 drugs do have side effects, including muscle loss. That is one reason they might not help most people, says Horvath, who says he isn’t ready to take the drugs himself, although he’s thought about it. “Clearly, the drugs have many benefits in obese people, but I did not yet see sufficient evidence that they will benefit skinny people,” he said. “I guess we will have to wait.” Answers could start to emerge in a year or two. This past January, a US agency, ARPA-H, put $38 million toward a study in Texas that will attempt to determine whether semaglutide has anti-aging effects in healthy people over 60. That research will look at changes in cognition, mobility, and acuity of the senses. That project also seeks to help define a regulatory pathway for anti-aging drugs and to “build a new therapeutic industry” around longevity. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. Yuancheng (Ryan) Lu is behind one of the buzziest results in rejuvenation science. “Xenocortical mice” are a dramatic demonstration of organoid technologies Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Station F and Anthropic say the AI boom is drawing startups back in person
2026-10-08

Station F and Anthropic say the AI boom is drawing startups back in person

The Next Web | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Aiden Blake, head of startup sales EMEA at Anthropic, speaking at Wave by Vento in Turin AI makes it possible to build a company from anywhere, but founders are still choosing to sit together, Station F director Roxanne Varza and Anthropic’s Aiden Blake said. They spoke on a panel at Wave by Vento in Turin on Thursday. “What’s really interesting is we’re seeing more and more people coming since the ‘AI boom’. I think actually a lot of teams are really embracing physical presence,” Varza said. Station F, in Paris, always has 1,000 early-stage companies on site, Varza said. Blake is head of startup sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa at Anthropic. Charlie Perreau of Les Echos moderated the session, titled “The Power of Proximity”. “The ability to build a product and to engage with prospective customers online has never been easier. What that actually means for people is that the real trust, I think, happens in person,” Blake said. Station F launched F/ai, its programme for AI startups, in February. Its partners include Anthropic, OpenAI, Mistral AI and Meta. Teams can be hybrid, Varza said, but they must come to Station F for certain moments of the programme. Without that, she said, they do not get the momentum they need. About two-thirds of the current batch is not French, Varza said. It includes teams from Asia, Stockholm, the UK and Poland. Blake said Anthropic joined because it wants to be present in the startup community. Google, Notion and Anthropic, his last three employers, have all been Station F partners, he said. Asked whether proximity is about people, information or serendipity, Varza said it was all three. Many founders today are repeat founders with large networks, she said, and being in the room with them opens doors. She said relationships cannot be engineered, only influenced, and much is best left to chance. Blake said founders at Anthropic’s events want to meet other builders with the same problems. They also want to hear from people further along. He named access to capital as part of proximity too, and pointed to Stockholm and Warsaw as cities where new companies are emerging. At the same event, Bending Spoons’ Luca Ferrari said capital is no longer Europe’s big barrier. Anthropic supports startups across Europe, the Middle East and Africa from Dublin, where 150 people work across several teams, Blake said. He said the city offers talent and a time zone that covers markets from South Africa to France and Germany. Varza said a great company can now be built from anywhere, but coming together physically still matters. Blake said ElevenLabs started fully remote and opened offices as it grew. Last month it opened one in Brussels. Many people who have worked remotely want to be back around teammates, Blake said, especially in go-to-market roles, where they learn from each other. Not every company agrees. In August, Dropbox’s new co-CEO said remote work is staying at the company. Asked what will give a startup its edge in five years, Varza said the tech stack matters. So will how fast founders build relationships, she said. Blake said he finds it hard to predict even five days ahead. The startups that succeed, he said, will be clear about the problem they solve and keep listening to what customers care about. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

AI model evaluator Arena nearly doubles its valuation to $3.1B
2026-10-08

AI model evaluator Arena nearly doubles its valuation to $3.1B

The Next Web | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Arena has raised $200M at a $3.1B valuation and is launching an Alignment Index that ranks AI models on how often they act without authorisation or report work they never finished. It measures that the same way it measures preference, through its users, which is a harder thing to crowdsource. Arena has raised $200M at a $3.1B valuation and will start ranking AI models on how often they do things nobody asked them to. Lightspeed Venture Partners and Khosla Ventures led the round. The company was worth $1.7B in January. Its Alignment Index tracks signals including how often a model takes an unauthorised action, and how often it tells a user it finished a task it did not complete, chief executive Anastasios Angelopoulos told Bloomberg. It covers more than two dozen models. OpenAI’s GPT-6.1 Sol is first and Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5.5 second. Arena began in 2023 as Chatbot Arena, a research project at Berkeley’s Sky Computing Lab, incorporated in 2025 and reported $100M in annualised revenue by June on more than 10 million human evaluations. Salesforce Ventures and Dell Technologies Capital joined the round. When it was LMArena raising $150M in January, we noted that crowdsourced voting can be gamed where safeguards are weak, and can reward answers that sound right over answers that are. Which of two replies you prefer is a matter of taste. Whether an agent did something nobody authorised is not. Google DeepMind set 100 agents to prove 71 conjectures in September and 14% cheated the grader, redefining a theorem’s symbols so unproven statements became trivially true. The swarm reported all 71 solved. Arena’s index is also a grader. The Information Commissioner’s Office opened a call for evidence on Thursday on how organisations manage data protection risks from agents, with responses due on 20 November, feeding a statutory code of practice. Autonomy “is not an excuse for poor compliance“, its director of technology regulation Richard Nevinson said. Europe’s version of Arena is smaller and points somewhere else. Galtea, spun out of the Barcelona Supercomputing Center, raised $3.2M in March to generate adversarial test cases against an agent’s intended behaviour and hand the results to compliance teams. It sells them as evidence for the EU AI Act, where breaches reach EUR 35M. Both companies measure the same thing. They sell different objects. One produces a ranking that model makers want to win, which is why they turn up. The other produces a document a regulator will ask to see. Arena is worth nine hundred and seventy times what Galtea raised. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Odyssey-3 drives a car in India and runs humanoids from one world model
2026-10-08

Odyssey-3 drives a car in India and runs humanoids from one world model

The Next Web | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Flexion’s humanoid robot running on Odyssey’s world model Odyssey has launched Odyssey-3, a world model that the Palo Alto AI lab says can control robot arms and humanoids, drive a car, fly drones and play video games. A research preview is available now, the company said on Thursday. World models learn from large amounts of visual data to predict how a scene will change next, including how actions affect what happens. Odyssey says its new model gives machines a general understanding of the world before they learn a specific task. “Odyssey-3 is a big step toward a single intelligence that can understand and operate in the world around us,” said Oliver Cameron, Odyssey’s co-founder and chief executive. Odyssey-3 Pro scored 66.1 on the video-to-video test of Physics-IQ Verified, the highest score reported on that leaderboard, according to Odyssey. That score uses the best of eight attempts per task. The benchmark, from Anates Labs and DeepMind, asks models to continue videos of real physics experiments. The comparison includes models from Nvidia and Black Forest Labs. Odyssey also says the model ranked first in three of the four categories of the WorldMark benchmark. That result comes from the company’s own evaluation. Odyssey showed the model in six areas. With tens of hours of robot demonstrations, it completed tasks with robot arms. It also recovered from errors that were not in those demonstrations, such as a missed grasp. The humanoid robot company Flexion has built control policies on Odyssey-3. Odyssey says they handled lighting changes that caused the tested baselines to fail. The company also drove a car on real roads in India with a driving policy trained on about 20 hours of data. Odyssey-3 also guided drones indoors, generated environments for training AI agents and played Grand Theft Auto V. Self-driving car veterans founded Odyssey. In June it raised $310 million at a $1.45 billion valuation, with Amazon and AMD Ventures among the investors. Odyssey is one of several companies building world models. Last month AMD agreed to buy Fei-Fei Li’s World Labs startup for $8.2bn in stock. In September, startup Emulate, founded by former DeepMind world-model researchers, was reportedly in talks to raise $700m. Nvidia offers its own open Cosmos world models. Google launches workplace AI agent that gets its own email address

Google launches workplace AI agent that gets its own email address

The Next Web | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Google’s new Gemini agent gives each coworker agent its own Workspace account, email address, calendar and entry in the company directory, and runs work for days after the laptop closes. Every action is logged to the agent rather than to a person, which is the part European regulators have spent the year asking about. Google is giving AI agents their own email addresses. The Gemini agent it announced on Thursday creates coworker agents that hold a Workspace account, a calendar, a Drive and an entry in the company directory. They act under their own identity rather than the employee’s. Each gets a cryptographically attested identity, stamped into its logs and into any virtual machine it starts, Google Cloud chief executive Thomas Kurian wrote in the announcement. Administrators approve role-based permissions. All traffic passes through what Google calls an AI network firewall. The agent works inline in Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Slides, Chat and Calendar, and also in Microsoft 365 and Slack. Work that takes hours or days keeps running after the laptop closes. It routes jobs across Google’s own models and Anthropic’s Claude. One line in the governance section matters more than the rest. Every action is written to an audit trail attributed to the agent, not a person. Google launched Gemini Enterprise in April as a product line that built and ran agents, and this is the first time it has given one a staff pass. Britain’s regulator was asking who answers for them that morning. Hours earlier the Information Commissioner’s Office had named Google among ten developers it secured data protection changes from, and opened a call for evidence on agent risks, with responses due on 20 November. The fact agents act with autonomy “is not an excuse for poor compliance“, its director of technology regulation Richard Nevinson said. Companies already cannot count the agents they have. NeuralTrust in Barcelona raised EUR 17.2M in June to map agent sprawl inside large organisations, and says about 1.2% of the interactions it inspects daily are malicious. Gartner expects 40% of enterprises to pull autonomous agents back by 2027 over governance gaps. Google’s answer is to make them findable. Its post names Deutsche Telekom as a European data sovereignty customer, though it gives no regions, no pricing and no general availability date. The Commission’s proposed Cloud and AI Development Act would put sensitive public workloads behind EU ownership requirements that Google Cloud cannot currently meet. There is one thing the post does not cover. It explains at length how an administrator grants an agent its permissions, maps its identity through OAuth into external systems and caps its spending. It does not say how you take any of it away. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week.

Pentagon offers PsiQuantum a conditional loan of up to $150m
2026-10-08

Pentagon offers PsiQuantum a conditional loan of up to $150m

The Next Web | 2026-10-08

Visit Original Website
Cryogenic cabinets at PsiQuantum’s PsiFactory in Milpitas, California PsiQuantum has received a conditional commitment for a loan of up to $150 million from the US Department of War’s Office of Strategic Capital. The quantum computing company announced the commitment on Thursday. It is the second conditional loan from the same Pentagon office in two days. On Wednesday, the Office of Strategic Capital offered chipmaker Wolfspeed up to $1.5bn. “Today’s announcement shows the U.S. government’s bold leadership and continued commitment to quantum computing as a critical technology for America’s economic prosperity and national security,” said Victor Peng, PsiQuantum’s chief executive. The PsiQuantum loan would go to PsiFactory, PsiQuantum’s 140,000-square-foot hub in Milpitas, California. There, the company develops and tests the prototype systems behind its planned fault-tolerant quantum computers. PsiQuantum would add its own investment. The loan would buy test equipment for components and systems, infrastructure for assembly and testing, and advanced cryogenic systems for the prototypes, according to PsiQuantum. PsiQuantum says the financing is not guaranteed. A final loan will only follow if the company meets all the financial, legal, technical and regulatory conditions. In July, PsiQuantum signed a $125 million expanded agreement with DARPA under its Quantum Benchmarking Initiative. The programme tests whether anyone can build a useful quantum computer by 2033. PsiQuantum and Microsoft were already in its final stage when DARPA added four more teams on Wednesday. In September, PsiQuantum finalised a $100 million award from the US Department of Commerce under the CHIPS and Science Act. It said the money would fund work on optical switches, single-photon detectors and packaging. The company has also worked with the Air Force Research Lab since 2022. Outside the US, PsiQuantum broke ground in Australia in June, at Moreton Bay in Queensland. In September 2025, it broke ground in Chicago at the Illinois Quantum and Microelectronics Park.
